## 1. Question

Kodeebo's Windows cashier app works from a local, whole-file-encrypted SQLite store and syncs in both directions with the hosted multi-tenant server, which is the source of truth. Principle 1 requires that money and session time are never lost and never counted twice; principle 2 that the counter never waits, with N5 subscription control as its one named exception after a long, known offline grace period. Tier 1 has one cashier device per branch, devices never talk on the LAN, and nothing chosen now may block several devices per branch later. This note informs the decisions the founder takes through ADRs (and `/license-design` for N5): **(1)** adopt an existing sync engine or build a custom outbox protocol (Part A); **(2)** the device-to-server write path — idempotency, ordering and atomicity, delivery and failure handling (Part B); **(3)** the conflict policy per data class, how server changes reach the device, and the Tier 1 requirements that keep multi-device open (Part C); **(4)** how session time is measured and billed, the N5 signed-lease trust protocol and clock-skew handling at sync (Part D); and **(5)** approval of the `/prototype` sync spike with fault injection (network cut mid-close, resend, duplicate replay and more) that brief risk 1 requires before any sync ADR is accepted (Part E). The research is neutral on the desktop runtime (Compose Multiplatform Desktop, Electron or Tauri; desktop spike pending) and on the backend language (Go or Ktor; `/setup-stack` has not run). Distribution is hosted only, so the L1–L4 bundle rules do not apply, but any engine licence that restricts offering the engine inside a hosted service we sell is reported as licence-blocked. The encrypted store, key custody and Windows time sources are reused from [desktop-client-technology.md](desktop-client-technology.md) (desktop-note F101–F120, § 4.3.2 4a–4d), not re-researched.

| F3 | PowerSync Kotlin SDK targets Android, Apple, and JVM/desktop (Linux AArch64/X64, macOS AArch64/X64, Windows X64); the page gives no stable/beta label for it; web (JS/Wasm) is experimental and "not ready for production use". SQLDelight and Room can run queries on it; a custom SQLite driver is not covered on the page. | [PowerSync Kotlin SDK reference](https://docs.powersync.com/client-sdks/reference/kotlin.md) | 2 | Version-pinned page not available (page tracks "latest"); the page lists no JVM-specific limitations |
| F4 | SDK status labels on the overview page: Tauri = Alpha, .NET = Beta, Rust = Beta, Capacitor = Beta; JavaScript Web, Node.js, Kotlin, Swift, Flutter, React Native carry no label. Electron is not mentioned. | [Client SDKs overview](https://docs.powersync.com/client-sdks/overview.md) | 2 | Electron: use the Node.js or Web SDK is an inference, not sourced |
| F5 | PowerSync write path is row-level, not client-defined mutations: local writes to PowerSync tables add `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` entries to the `ps_crud` upload queue; the SDK calls the app's `uploadData()` which sends them to the app's OWN backend API; the backend must apply them to the source database synchronously (the client advances to the next checkpoint after the upload is processed). | [Writing client changes](https://docs.powersync.com/handling-writes/writing-client-changes.md), [Client-side integration](https://docs.powersync.com/configuration/app-backend/client-side-integration.md) | 2 | Consequence: the engine does not decide what a "sale" or "session charge" means; the backend upload handler must |
| F6 | PowerSync consistency: designed for causal+ consistency (the docs say it was tested with Jepsen); a checkpoint is a point-in-time snapshot including only fully committed transactions, applied only when all its data is downloaded; while the upload queue holds mutations the client does not advance to a new checkpoint, so it never resolves conflicts locally. The upload queue is a blocking FIFO: a mutation the server never acknowledges can stall the queue and stop the client from receiving new checkpoints. | [Consistency](https://docs.powersync.com/architecture/consistency.md) | 2 | Jepsen report itself not fetched: NOT SOURCEABLE - not read; tier-2 statement only |
| F7 | PowerSync retries: `uploadData()` is called after local writes, on connect/reconnect and on each keepalive; if it throws, the SDK waits `retryDelayMs` (default 5 s) and retries from the start of the queue; 4xx/5xx responses cause indefinite retry that blocks the queue; the docs advise returning 2xx with error details for validation/conflict failures. Idempotency of retried uploads is NOT stated on the pages fetched, nor explicit FIFO order (implied by "front of the queue"). | [Client-side integration](https://docs.powersync.com/configuration/app-backend/client-side-integration.md), [Writing client changes](https://docs.powersync.com/handling-writes/writing-client-changes.md) | 2 | Idempotency/exactly-once must therefore be built in the backend handler (idempotency key per mutation); "Handling write/validation errors" page not fetched: NOT SOURCEABLE - not read |
| F8 | PowerSync conflict handling: no automatic resolution for writes; backend validation and conflict handling are the developer's responsibility (constraint failures can occur long after an offline write); strategies listed: relax constraints, block the queue and monitor errors, park failed transactions server-side, or discard with notification. | [Consistency](https://docs.powersync.com/architecture/consistency.md) | 2 | Matches the Kodeebo need only if money rules are enforced in the handler |
| F9 | PowerSync source databases: Postgres 11+ (logical replication, `wal_level = logical`, publication `powersync`), MongoDB 6.0+ (change streams with post-images), MySQL 5.7+ (Beta), SQL Server 2019+ / Azure SQL (Beta); Azure DocumentDB alpha; Convex experimental. The bucket-storage database the service itself needs: NOT SOURCEABLE - the setup page does not name it, deeper architecture page not fetched. | [Source database setup](https://docs.powersync.com/configuration/source-db/setup.md) | 2 | Postgres 18.6 (our pin) vs "11 or later": the page names no upper bound; run: PENDING - S2 |
| F10 | PowerSync Service licence is the Functional Source License 1.1 with Apache-2.0 future licence (FSL-1.1-ALv2). It bars a "Competing Use": a commercial product or service that replaces the Software, replaces another product the licensor offers using it, or offers substantially similar functionality. Internal use is permitted, and each version converts to Apache-2.0 on its second anniversary. | [powersync-service LICENSE](https://github.com/powersync-ja/powersync-service/blob/main/LICENSE) (fetched via raw.githubusercontent.com) | 3 | Clause: "A Competing Use means making the Software available to others in a commercial product or service that:". Running it as an internal part of Kodeebo is not a sync-engine product, but whether Kodeebo counts as "essentially the same functionality" is a legal reading: needs counsel, **closed by founder statement (2026-10-18)**. Not licence-blocked on its face. |
| F11 | PowerSync Kotlin SDK encrypts via SQLite3MultipleCiphers (not SQLCipher) since 1.9.0: replace `com.powersync:core` with `com.powersync:common` and add `com.powersync:sqlite3multipleciphers`; factories `AndroidEncryptedDatabaseFactory`, `JavaEncryptedDatabaseFactory` (JVM), `NativeEncryptedDatabaseFactory`. JS Web SDK uses SQLite3MC (ChaCha20 default); Node.js SDK uses `better-sqlite3-multiple-ciphers`. Tauri, .NET and Rust: not covered ("planned"). No Windows-specific notes. | [Data encryption](https://docs.powersync.com/client-sdks/advanced/data-encryption.md) | 2 | Fits SQLite3MC option of desktop note F110; SQLCipher file-compat question (S10 there) not needed if SQLite3MC is chosen. Windows run: PENDING - S3 |

We must run the server component ourselves if we self-host, else use PowerSync Cloud. | [Self-hosting](https://docs.powersync.com/intro/self-hosting.md), [pricing](https://www.powersync.com/pricing) | 2 / 3 | Licence of the Open Edition is not named on the page (it is FSL per F10) |

| F20 | Zero writes: a mutator runs first on the client against the local datastore (optimistic), then Zero sends it to our push endpoint, which runs the server-side mutator in a database transaction and records that it ran; the server can add permission checks. Row changes flow back from the database to `zero-cache` by logical replication. If a mutator throws it is skipped and its optimistic change reverted; 5xx are retried up to four times by zero-cache. The page does not describe ordering, mutation IDs or idempotency, does not say whether writes queue offline (it says local writes go to IndexedDB and queued mutations retry after reconnect), and does not name the client storage engine beyond that. | [Zero writing data](https://zero.rocicorp.dev/docs/mutators), [Zero introduction](https://zero.rocicorp.dev/docs/introduction) | 2 | Client store is IndexedDB per the page, so no whole-file-encrypted SQLite. ### 3.2 Part B — write path: idempotency, ordering and atomicity, delivery

Tier E rows cite files under `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/` prefixed `b-`.

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F101 | **IETF Idempotency-Key header status (B1).** The work is Internet-Draft `draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header`, current revision -07 dated 2025-10-15, in the `httpapi` working group. The datatracker shows it as "Expired & archived", IESG state "I-D Exists" (IESG has not started processing it, or stopped without publication), no intended standards level set, no shepherd or responsible AD listed, 17 history events from 2021-07-01 (-00) to 2026-04-18 (the expiry event). Authors: Jena and Dalal. It is NOT an RFC. It replaces `draft-idempotency-header`. | [datatracker doc page](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header/) (fetched 2026-10-16); [datatracker API, document](https://datatracker.ietf.org/api/v1/doc/document/draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header/); [API, history](https://datatracker.ietf.org/api/v1/doc/dochistory/?doc__name=draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header&limit=50); [API, state 150](https://datatracker.ietf.org/api/v1/doc/state/150/) | 3 | Official IETF record, but an Internet-Draft is not a normative standard: use it as a design reference, not as a requirement. Header name is stable across revisions only as a draft; no RFC number exists. |
| F102 | **Draft -07 content.** The header is an Item Structured Header (RFC 8941) whose value is a String; a UUID or similar random id is recommended, uniqueness is defined by the resource owner. Missing key on an operation that requires it: 400 (problem details or `Link rel="describedby"`). Same key with a different payload: 422. Original request still in progress: 409. Replay after completion: return the result of the first completed operation, success or error. Expiry: the resource MAY purge keys and should publish its policy; no duration is given. No per-user or per-client scoping rule; security section advises a composite server-side lookup key, validating the key before use, and warns that low-entropy keys let an attacker read another client's cached response. A payload fingerprint is optional and server-generated. Not covered: key leakage in logs, key-exhaustion DoS, rate limiting. | [draft -07 text](https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header-07.txt) (fetched 2026-10-16; summarised by the fetch tool, not read in full by this researcher) | 3 | Internet-Draft, expired: a lead for the response contract (400/409/422/replay-original), not a standard. The draft says nothing about retention for devices offline for days. The 422-on-different-payload and 409-in-progress codes are the draft's; Kodeebo's contract may differ and must map errors to RFC 9457. |
| F103 | **PostgreSQL 18, Read Committed and `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT`.** `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` guarantees that each proposed row inserts or updates (barring unrelated errors). `DO NOTHING` can skip a row because of a concurrent transaction whose effects are not visible to the statement snapshot, which happens only in Read Committed. `UPDATE`/`DELETE`/`SELECT FOR UPDATE` wait for a concurrent updater, then re-evaluate the `WHERE` clause against the new row version. | [PG 18: Transaction Isolation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/transaction-iso.html) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 2 | Pinned major 18 (`pins.yaml` postgres.image 18.6, patch release not re-read). Consequence: a dedupe "insert-first" must be followed by a read that sees the winner's row; see F105. |
| F104 | **PostgreSQL 18, Repeatable Read / Serializable.** Repeatable Read fails an updater whose target row was changed by a transaction committed after it began ("could not serialize access due to concurrent update") and the docs say to retry the whole transaction. Serializable adds read/write-dependency detection, failures always use SQLSTATE 40001, applications must have a generic retry for them, read-only transactions never conflict at Repeatable Read, and unique violations from overlapping inserts can still occur at Serializable even after a pre-check `SELECT`. | [PG 18: Transaction Isolation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/transaction-iso.html) | 2 | Serializable for the whole write path means a retry loop on 40001 in every command handler; it is not needed if a per-device lock serialises the work (F107, F108). |
| F105 | **`INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` in PG 18.** `DO UPDATE` "guarantees an atomic INSERT or UPDATE outcome" under high concurrency. `DO NOTHING` skips a conflicting row and `RETURNING` returns only rows actually inserted or updated, so a skipped (duplicate) row yields no returned row; the caller must run a separate lookup to find the original result. A `DO UPDATE ... WHERE` that is false still locks the row but does not return it. `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` on the unique index can make `ON CONFLICT` fail unexpectedly with a unique violation. | [PG 18: INSERT](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-insert.html) | 2 | Basis for the inbox pattern: `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, device_id, command_id) DO NOTHING`, then `SELECT` the stored response on zero rows inserted. The pattern is an inference from these semantics, tested in PENDING S101. |
| F106 | **Row locks and deadlocks in PG 18.** Row-level locks block only writers and other lockers of the same row; plain reads are not blocked; locks last to transaction end. `FOR UPDATE` blocks other lockers and updaters. PostgreSQL detects deadlocks and aborts one transaction; the docs advise taking locks in a consistent order and retrying the aborted transaction, and warn that a long-open transaction waiting on a lock can wait indefinitely absent a deadlock. | [PG 18: Explicit Locking](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/explicit-locking.html) | 2 | A per-device "cursor" row locked `FOR UPDATE` is a documented way to serialise one device's applies (F108). Lock-wait timeouts (`lock_timeout`) were not read: NOT SOURCEABLE in this part — turn budget. |
| F107 | **Advisory locks in PG 18.** Application-defined locks the system does not enforce. Session-level locks ignore transaction semantics (a lock taken in a rolled-back transaction is still held; requests stack); transaction-level locks release automatically at commit or rollback. They share the lock-table memory pool sized by `max_locks_per_transaction` and `max_connections`. Using an advisory-lock call in a query with `LIMIT` can lock unexpected rows because evaluation order is not guaranteed. | [PG 18: Explicit Locking, advisory locks](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/explicit-locking.html) | 2 | Prefer transaction-level locks so a crashed handler cannot leak a lock when pooled connections are used. Pooler (PgBouncer) interaction: NOT SOURCEABLE — not searched; matters only for session-level locks. |
| F108 | **Advisory lock functions in PG 18.** `pg_advisory_xact_lock(key bigint)` or `(int4, int4)` waits for an exclusive lock; `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock` returns false immediately instead of waiting; transaction-level locks have "no provision for manual release" and free at transaction end; the one-bigint and two-int4 key spaces do not overlap. | [PG 18: Admin functions, advisory lock functions](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/functions-admin.html) | 2 | The 64-bit key is a hash of (tenant, device) with a collision risk that must be handled; a lock row in a table (F106) has no collision. The hash-collision probability is arithmetic, not sourced. |
| F109 | **`FOR UPDATE` clauses in PG 18.** `NOWAIT` errors instead of waiting; `SKIP LOCKED` skips locked rows and the docs say it "provides an inconsistent view of the data", suitable for queue-like tables with multiple consumers but "not suitable for general purpose work". With `ORDER BY ... LIMIT` in Read Committed, locking happens after sorting, so rows can come back out of order or fewer than expected; Repeatable Read and Serializable raise 40001 instead. | [PG 18: SELECT, locking clause](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-select.html) | 2 | Consequence for ordering: a worker that applies "the next unapplied command of device X" with `SKIP LOCKED` is safe only if exactly one worker per device can hold the device lock; a global queue with `SKIP LOCKED` does not give per-device order by itself. |
| F110 | **Row-level security and uniqueness in PG 18.** Referential-integrity checks (unique, primary key, foreign key) "always bypass row security". Superusers and `BYPASSRLS` roles bypass policies; table owners bypass unless `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` is set; a policy without `WITH CHECK` applies its `USING` expression to new rows. The page did not cover `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` interaction with policies or the `current_setting` tenant pattern. | [PG 18: Row Security Policies](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/ddl-rowsecurity.html) | 2 | Two consequences: (1) a unique index on the idempotency key alone is global across tenants and leaks existence of another tenant's key through a conflict error, so the unique index must lead with `tenant_id`; (2) `CREATE POLICY` reference not read: NOT SOURCEABLE — turn budget, not reached; a partial run is F128, the full policy matrix is PENDING — S106. |
| F111 | **SQLite transactions.** `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` starts a write transaction at once and fails with `SQLITE_BUSY` if another connection already writes; a default (DEFERRED) transaction that reads first and then writes can fail with `SQLITE_BUSY` at the upgrade; only one write transaction is active at a time while readers keep a snapshot; `COMMIT` may return `SQLITE_BUSY` and can be retried; BEGIN does not nest (use `SAVEPOINT`); for some errors (`SQLITE_FULL`, `SQLITE_IOERR`, ...) SQLite may have to roll back the whole transaction. "EXCLUSIVE and IMMEDIATE are the same in WAL mode." | [SQLite: BEGIN TRANSACTION](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_transaction.html) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 2 | Basis for the client outbox: business rows and the outbox row go in ONE `BEGIN IMMEDIATE ... COMMIT`; a sender thread reads outbox rows in separate short read transactions. SQLite version at the pin: not pinned (desktop-note F102/F106: bundle >= 3.51.3). |
| F112 | **SQLite rowid and AUTOINCREMENT.** Without AUTOINCREMENT a new rowid is usually max+1 and deleted ids "might be reused" and may not ascend; with `AUTOINCREMENT` automatically chosen ids are monotonically increasing and never reused, tracked in `sqlite_sequence`, but "not that they will be sequential": a failed insert (for example a uniqueness violation) can consume an id, while ids from a rolled-back transaction are ignored and reusable. At the 64-bit max, inserts fail with `SQLITE_FULL`. | [SQLite: AUTOINCREMENT](https://www.sqlite.org/autoinc.html) | 2 | Consequence: an `AUTOINCREMENT` outbox id is monotonic but can have gaps (a gap-free per-device sequence needs its own counter row incremented in the same transaction; behaviour tested in tier E, F121). |
| F113 | **SQLite atomic commit and its storage assumption.** A transaction is all-or-nothing across power loss or OS crash in rollback-journal mode (WAL mode uses a different mechanism); this relies on `fsync` not returning before data is durable, and the page warns that flush primitives are broken on some Windows and Linux versions and some disks report data written while it is in a volatile cache; SQLite cannot detect that. A hot journal must not be deleted or renamed. | [SQLite: Atomic Commit](https://www.sqlite.org/atomiccommit.html) | 2 | Same warning as desktop-note F105/F106 (not re-researched). For B2/B3: after a power cut the local outbox can lose its most recent commits (`synchronous=NORMAL` in WAL) or, on a lying disk, more; the server cursor must therefore never be assumed behind the device (see F123, restore detection). |
| F114 | **HTTP idempotent methods (RFC 9110 s9.2.2).** A method is idempotent if multiple identical requests have the same intended effect on the server as one; PUT, DELETE and safe methods are. A client SHOULD NOT automatically retry a non-idempotent method (POST) unless it has some means to know the semantics are idempotent or to detect that the original was never applied. A server may still log each request separately. | [RFC 9110](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110.html) (text read from rfc9110.txt, fetched 2026-10-16) | 1 | Justifies carrying a client-generated id on every command so that a POST is safe to retry. |
| F115 | **Status codes and Retry-After (RFC 9110, RFC 6585).** `Retry-After` is either an HTTP-date or a non-negative number of seconds, and can accompany 503 and 3xx (RFC 9110 s10.2.3). 429 means too many requests in a given time and MAY carry `Retry-After` (RFC 6585 s4). 401 means the request "has not been applied" for lack of valid credentials, MUST carry `WWW-Authenticate`, and the client MAY repeat it with new credentials (s15.5.2). 409 means a conflict with the current state of the target resource that the user might resolve (s15.5.10). 413 Content Too Large lets the server refuse oversize content and SHOULD carry `Retry-After` if temporary (s15.5.14). 422 means syntactically correct content the server could not process (s15.5.21). | [RFC 9110](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110.html); [RFC 6585](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6585.html) | 1 | RFC 9110 states for 401 that the request was not applied, which is the property a sync client needs when its token expires mid-batch (section 4, B3). |
| F116 | **Problem Details (RFC 9457).** Errors use a JSON object (media type `application/problem+json`) with members `type`, `status`, `title`, `detail`, `instance` plus extension members. Section 3.1 defines the members; only the section headings were read here, not the member texts. | [RFC 9457](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9457.html) (headings read from rfc9457.txt, fetched 2026-10-16) | 1 | Needed because `coding-standards.md` maps errors to RFC 9457 at the HTTP edge. Member semantics: NOT SOURCEABLE in this part (headings only read); the `api-standards.md` of the repo governs. |
| F117 | **Exponential backoff with jitter (AWS Architecture Blog).** The post compares Full Jitter (randomise the whole sleep), Equal Jitter (keep part fixed, randomise the rest) and Decorrelated Jitter (the next ceiling grows from the previous sleep). It reports Full and Equal make about the same number of calls, Decorrelated more; Full does less work but takes slightly longer than Decorrelated, Equal takes much longer; jitter cuts total work substantially under contention but does not change the "N2 nature" of the work. The formulas are images in the page and were not captured. | [Exponential Backoff And Jitter](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/exponential-backoff-and-jitter/) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 4 | Maintainer-grade engineering writing (AWS), corroborated by nothing in this part. Formulas: NOT SOURCEABLE (images only); the simplest well-known form is not quoted from memory. The numbers (base, cap) are config values, not hardcoded. |
| F118 | **Stripe idempotent requests (API v1).** The client generates the key (V4 UUID suggested, up to 255 characters, no sensitive data). Stripe saves the status code and body of the first request once execution begins, and retries with the same key return that saved response, including 500 errors. Parameters are compared with the original and a mismatch errors. Keys may be pruned automatically after at least 24 hours, and a reused pruned key creates a new request. Validation failures and concurrent-execution conflicts are NOT saved, so those can be retried. Only POST accepts keys. API v2 differs: successful operations are not repeated and failed or partly failed work is retried to completion. | [Stripe: Idempotent requests](https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 4 | Vendor documentation of a different product, used as design precedent only. Stripe's 24-hour window fits an always-online client, NOT a device offline for days: Kodeebo's window must be derived from N5 (section 5 and open question Q1). |

After the rejection the cursor advanced and the previously gapped seq 3 applied. | `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/b-s101-setup.sql`, `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/b-s101-run.sh`, output `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/b-s101-run.output.txt` (section T1); container `postgres:18.6-alpine3.23` (reported `PostgreSQL 18.6 on x86_64-pc-linux-musl`), 2026-10-16 | E | Probe schema, not product code. Money amounts were integer piasters. |
| F125 | **Executed: a concurrent duplicate waits, it does not race.** Session A held an open transaction for 3 s after applying command `a1`; session B sent the same command; B returned after about 2.1 s with `duplicate_seq:applied`. B blocked on the cursor row update (F103/F106 behaviour), then re-evaluated and found the inbox row. | same files as F124 (section T2) | E | So "request still in progress" (the draft's 409, F102) is produced by waiting, not by an error, unless the server sets `lock_timeout` or uses `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock`: lock timeouts NOT SOURCEABLE — not read. |
| F126 | **Executed: per-device order under concurrent retries.** 30 concurrent psql sessions per round, 10 rounds, sent seq 1 to 5 six times each in shuffled order (retries after gaps). Final cursor 5; exactly 5 `apply_log` rows with 5 distinct seq; the identity column `global_order` ascended with `device_seq` (values 5 to 9: the identity sequence also advanced for earlier test rows, so identity numbers are not gap-free). The ordering used one compare-and-set `UPDATE ... WHERE last_applied_seq = seq - 1`, no explicit lock statement and no Serializable. | same files as F124 (section T3) | E | The run did not count how many `gap` and `duplicate` replies occurred and did not test Serializable, advisory locks or a second server process: PENDING — S103. Throughput was not measured. |
| F127 | **Executed: a rejected command does not block later ones.** Inside the apply function a failing business effect (CHECK violation) was caught by a sub-block, an inbox row with outcome `rejected` and the reason was written, and the cursor still advanced, so seq 3 applied right after the rejected seq 2. | same files as F124 (section T1) | E | Shows the mechanism only. What the business should do with a rejected money command is a decision (section 9, Q4). |
| F128 | **Executed: tenant scoping of the key with RLS.** With role `kodeebo_app` (no bypass) and a tenant from a session setting: a unique key on `command_id` alone made tenant 2's insert fail with a unique-violation error naming the constraint, which tells tenant 2 that another tenant used that id (with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` it returned 0 rows silently); RLS hid tenant 1's row from tenant 2's SELECT. With the tenant-leading key `(tenant_id, device_id, command_id)` both tenants inserted the same command id without conflict. An insert carrying another tenant's `tenant_id`, and an `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` aimed at another tenant's row, both failed with "new row violates row-level security policy" and tenant 1's row was unchanged. | same files as F124 (section T4) | E | Agrees with F110. Run only for one INSERT policy shape; other policies untested (S106). |

### 3.3 Part C — conflict policy, server-to-device feed, multi-device readiness

Tier E rows cite scripts and output saved under `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/` (prefix `c-`). Tier 5 and lead-only rows are marked. Where a stack reference exists it is cited with its Last verified date.

#### [C] 3.1 C2: PostgreSQL feed mechanics (PostgreSQL 18 docs, pinned major)

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F201 | `nextval` values are not reclaimed when the calling transaction aborts, so sequences have gaps; the doc states sequences cannot give gapless numbers; `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` can also burn values; after a crash a sequence may recover to either its old or new state. The page does not say that values commit in issue order. | [PG 18 sequence functions](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/functions-sequence.html) | 2 | Matters twice: (a) a sequence is a bad feed cursor, (b) a sequence cannot give gap-free receipt or order numbers (those must be allocated by the device, see C1/C3) |
| F202 | Snapshot functions: `pg_current_snapshot()` returns a `pg_snapshot` with text form `xmin:xmax:xip_list`; every transaction id below `xmin` has finished (committed or rolled back); ids at or above `xmax` had not finished; `xip_list` holds the top-level ids still in progress. `pg_current_xact_id()` returns an `xid8`; `pg_visible_in_snapshot(xid8, pg_snapshot)` tests visibility. | [PG 18 snapshot functions](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/functions-info.html) | 2 | Basis for a "no in-flight writer below this value" cursor |
| F203 | `pg_xact_commit_timestamp` works only when `track_commit_timestamp` is on, only for transactions after it was enabled, and old values are removed by vacuum. It takes a 32-bit `xid`, not `xid8`. | same page as F202 | 2 | A commit timestamp is not a safe feed cursor: it is not retained, and ties are possible (not stated in the doc; inference) |
| F204 | Read Committed takes a snapshot per statement; Repeatable Read takes one for the whole transaction and fails with a serialization error when a concurrently committed change touches a row it wants to update, requiring retry of the whole transaction; snapshot isolation still allows write skew. | [PG 18 transaction isolation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/transaction-iso.html) | 2 | A feed reader should read inside one Repeatable Read read-only transaction (one snapshot for the page and the horizon) so the horizon and the rows agree. Server code that applies device batches must retry on SQLSTATE 40001 |
| F205 | Logical decoding: slots persist independently of connections, are crash-safe and retain WAL; each change is emitted once in normal operation but after a crash recent changes can be resent, so consumers must tolerate duplicates; one receiver at a time; an unconsumed slot keeps retaining WAL and in the extreme can force a shutdown to avoid transaction id wraparound; slots on standbys need `hot_standby_feedback` and can be invalidated; failover needs explicit slot synchronisation. | [PG 18 logical decoding concepts](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/logicaldecoding-explanation.html) | 2 | A slot is a server-side consumer for one reader, not a per-device cursor: thousands of devices cannot each hold a slot. Use it (if at all) to feed one fan-out service, not devices. The page does not state commit ordering of emitted changes: NOT SOURCEABLE on that page; part B/other parts may cover the output-plugin ordering |
| F206 | `NOTIFY` is delivered only after the sending transaction commits, between transactions on the listener; payload under 8000 bytes by default; identical notifications in one transaction collapse; delivery order follows commit order across transactions; the queue is about 8 GB and a full queue makes `NOTIFY` fail at commit (a stuck `LISTEN` session in a long transaction blocks cleanup). The page does not say what a disconnected listener misses. | [PG 18 NOTIFY](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-notify.html) | 2 | Good as a nudge ("something changed, pull"), never as the data path. Transaction-pooled PgBouncer cannot carry `LISTEN` (stack reference F207) |
| F207 | Stack reference: PgBouncer transaction pooling is incompatible with `LISTEN`, session advisory locks, `PREPARE` etc.; keep `LISTEN/NOTIFY` and advisory-lock leader election on a direct session-mode connection. RLS: `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` on every tenant table; the `app` role is `NOBYPASSRLS`; unique/PK/FK checks bypass RLS (a duplicate-key error reveals a hidden row). | `docs/stack-reference/postgres/best-practices.md` (Last verified 2026-10-11) | 2 (stack reference) | The unique-check bypass matters for device-supplied ids: a cross-tenant id collision must not be distinguishable. Use tenant-scoped composite keys |
| F208 | Executed probe, PostgreSQL 18.6 (`postgres:18.6-alpine3.23`), 2026-10-16: transaction A inserted id 1 and stayed open; transaction B inserted id 2 and committed; a reader with a naive cursor `id > 0` saw only id 2 and set cursor 2; after A committed, `id > 2` returned nothing, so id 1 was skipped for good. A horizon reader (rows tagged with `pg_current_xact_id()`, read only where `tx < pg_snapshot_xmin(pg_current_snapshot())`) returned nothing while A was open (held back, not lost) and then returned both rows (ids 1 and 2) on the next read. A rolled-back `nextval` was not reused. Snapshot at read 1 was `754:756:754`. | `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/c-cursor-probe.py`, output `c-cursor-probe.out`; command `python3 -I c-cursor-probe.py`; Docker image above; 2026-10-16 | E | Confirms F201/F202 at the pinned version. Side effect to plan for: any long transaction anywhere in the database (not only in the tenant) holds the horizon back and delays the feed for everyone; the feed needs a statement/transaction timeout policy and an alert on horizon age |
| F219 | Tombstones and resurrection (analogy from a replicated database): Cassandra keeps deletion markers for `gc_grace_seconds` (default 864000 s, ten days); a replica that stays disconnected longer than that can bring back deleted data, because the other nodes no longer hold the tombstone. | [Apache Cassandra docs, compaction overview](https://cassandra.apache.org/doc/latest/cassandra/managing/operating/compaction/overview.html) | 2 (docs; version not pinned, not a Kodeebo dependency) | Same failure mode for a device offline longer than the server's tombstone retention: it must re-bootstrap, not resume from its cursor |
| F226 | HTTP semantics (RFC 9110, read to character 480,000 of 564,890): 409 means the request clashes with the resource's current state and the client may fix and resubmit; 410 means the resource is gone, likely permanently; 412 means a precondition header evaluated false (optimistic concurrency with a validator); 426 means the server will not serve the request over the current protocol and must name the protocol to switch to. The idempotent-methods definition (9.2.2) was not in the part read. | [RFC 9110](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110.html) | 1 | 412 fits owner-dashboard edits (If-Match on a config version); 410 or a typed problem response fits "cursor too old, re-bootstrap"; 426 is defined for protocol switching, so using it for "client too old" is an adaptation, to be decided in the API contract |
| F227 | SQLite `PRAGMA user_version` is an integer in the database header (offset 60) that applications may use for their own data-format version; SQLite itself ignores it. `application_id` identifies the file type. | [SQLite pragma docs](https://www.sqlite.org/pragma.html) | 2 (SQLite version not pinned in this note; the engine is part A and the desktop note) | Natural place for the local schema version used when an old client meets a newer server |
| F228 | API versioning example: Stripe issues monthly API versions with no breaking changes and twice a year a major release with breaking changes; a request names its version in the `Stripe-Version` header and client libraries pin the version current at their release. | [Stripe API versioning](https://docs.stripe.com/api/versioning) | 2 (vendor docs) | Pattern only: version pinned by the client, additive changes between majors. Not a dependency |

#### [C] 3.2 C1: conflict policies in the literature

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F210 | Invariant confluence (I-confluence): a set of transactions can run without coordination and still converge while keeping an invariant if and only if the transactions are I-confluent with respect to it (Theorem 1 in the extended paper; the abstract calls it a necessary and sufficient condition). | [Bailis et al., Coordination Avoidance in Database Systems, arXiv 1402.2237 (extended version of PVLDB 8(3), 2015)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1402.2237); text read from the PDF at [arxiv.org/pdf/1402.2237](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1402.2237) | 4 (peer-reviewed paper; the protocol has no tier for papers) | Papers are treated as tier 4 (recognised engineering/academic writing) and corroborated by F211-F214; none is a standard |
| F211 | The paper's Table 2 (read from the PDF text): uniqueness with "choose a specific value" is NOT I-confluent, "choose some value" IS; `AUTO_INCREMENT` insert is NOT I-confluent; foreign-key insert is, foreign-key delete is NOT (cascading delete is); a counter with a greater-than threshold is I-confluent under increment but NOT under decrement; with a less-than threshold the reverse; set insertion/containment are. In TPC-C, "per-district order IDs are sequential" and "new order IDs are sequentially assigned" are listed as needing coordination, while ten of twelve TPC-C invariants are I-confluent. | same as F210 (Table 2 and the TPC-C table in the PDF) | 4 | Maps straight onto Kodeebo: daily order numbers and receipt numbers (sequential), stock that must stay >= 0 (decrement against a lower bound) and one open session per station (uniqueness of a specific value) are the three invariants that need coordination; ledger appends, payments and audit records (insert-only, foreign keys to existing rows) do not |
| F212 | Unique-id generation by partitioning the namespace per replica needs no coordination ("choose some value" is I-confluent when each replica draws from its own slice). | same as F210 (uniqueness section; I read the claim in the text near line 481-497 of the extracted PDF) | 4 | Supports device-prefixed numbers (device id + local counter) as the coordination-free numbering scheme; gap-free and human-sequential numbers are not obtainable this way (F211) |
| F213 | CALM theorem: programs that can be implemented consistently without coordination are exactly the monotonic ones (new information only adds to conclusions, nothing is retracted). | [Hellerstein and Alvaro, Keeping CALM, arXiv 1901.01930](https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.01930) | 4 | Append-only facts (ledger, payments, audit) are monotonic; "close the day", "void", "stock never below zero" are non-monotonic and need a single decider or a reservation |
| F214 | Bounded counters adapt escrow to enforce numeric invariants (for example a stock level that must stay non-negative) on eventually consistent stores; the abstract reports far lower latency than strong consistency. Mechanism, read from the PDF text: the gap between the counter value and its bound is a pool of "rights" (value 40 with bound n >= 10 gives 30 rights); rights are split among replicas; a replica may decrement only with rights it holds locally, otherwise the operation fails or it must obtain rights from another replica by a transfer operation; a replica never overstates its rights, so the bound holds. | [Balegas et al., arXiv 1503.09052](https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.09052); mechanism from [arxiv.org/pdf/1503.09052](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1503.09052) | 4 | Escrow only helps when replicas can talk to each other to transfer rights; Kodeebo devices never talk to each other, so a transfer would go via the server and is unavailable while offline: a device can only spend the rights it already holds. See stock row in section 4 |
| F215 | CRDT guarantee and its limit: the Ink and Switch essay says CRDTs merge concurrent edits automatically but cannot resolve two users changing the same property of the same object; they keep the conflicting values and leave the choice to the application. It lists open problems: unbounded history growth, no transport, schema evolution and mixed application versions. It also describes the server as a secondary "cloud peer", not the source of truth. | [Kleppmann, Wiggins, van Hardenberg, McGranaghan, Local-first software, Onward! 2019](https://www.inkandswitch.com/essay/local-first/) | 4 | Kodeebo's server-is-the-source-of-truth premise differs from the essay's model; use the essay for the conflict vocabulary only. The primary CRDT paper (Shapiro et al.) could not be fetched: see F216 |
| F216 | NOT SOURCEABLE - the primary CRDT papers (Shapiro, Preguica, Baquero, Zawirski: HAL inria-00609399 and inria-00555588 / RR-7506) returned an Anubis access-denied page; lip6 mirror refused the connection. Definitions of strong eventual consistency, LWW-Register, G-Counter/PN-Counter and OR-Set are therefore not cited from the primary text. | tried: hal.inria.fr/inria-00609399/document, inria.hal.science/inria-00555588/document, pages.lip6.fr/Marc.Shapiro/papers/RR-7687.pdf | - | Retry through another mirror (arXiv, ACM, crdt.tech) in a later run if the CRDT option is still alive after part A |
| F217 | Operation-based rebase / server reconciliation, as implemented by one product: the client applies named mutators locally and records them as pending with sequential ids; the server re-runs the same named mutators against authoritative data and returns the highest applied mutation id per client; the client rewinds to the last server state, applies the server patch and replays the still-pending mutations (like a git rebase); the server's result wins; an opaque cookie marks the server state the client has; a data-free "poke" over WebSocket or SSE tells clients to pull. | [Replicache docs, How it works](https://doc.replicache.dev/concepts/how-it-works) | 2 (vendor docs, version not pinned; not a candidate pin, engines are part A) | This is the pattern that matches brief F2 (device order, never twice: per-client mutation id) and the push-nudge-then-pull design in 3.3. The page does not state the product's maintenance status: NOT SOURCEABLE |
| F218 | Single-owner lease: a lease is a time-limited contract giving its holder rights over a datum; the holder controls writes during the term, so non-Byzantine failures cost performance, not correctness, and short terms limit the damage. Citation: Gray and Cheriton, SOSP 1989. The primary text could not be read (Stanford report host refused the connection); this row rests on a search-result summary. | search-result summary of [Gray and Cheriton, Leases, SOSP 1989](https://web.eecs.umich.edu/~mosharaf/Readings/Leases.pdf) (URL listed in the result, not fetched) | 4 as a lead only (search snippet; not read) | Lead, not basis for decision. A lease needs a clock both sides trust; Kodeebo's device clock is untrusted (desktop note F117, 4d) so a lease would be measured by the server and enforced at apply time |

#### [C] 3.3 C2: push transports (placeholder rows filled below as fetched)

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F220 | Server-sent events (`EventSource`): the browser reconnects automatically after a lost connection; the server may set the delay with a `retry` field; on reconnect the client sends the last seen event id in a `Last-Event-ID` header; HTTP 204 stops reconnection and any non-200 status or wrong content type fails the connection without reconnect; the spec recommends a comment line about every 15 seconds so legacy proxies do not drop an idle stream. | [WHATWG HTML Living Standard, Server-sent events](https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/server-sent-events.html) | 1 | `EventSource` is a browser API: a non-browser desktop client (any runtime) must implement the same reconnect and Last-Event-ID rules itself, or use another transport. Runtime is undecided: neutral |
| F221 | RFC 6202: long polling costs a held request per client and full HTTP headers per message; worst-case latency is above three network transits; proxies and servers may time out held requests (many sooner than the 300 s browser default; "generally 30 seconds is a safer value"); streaming can be defeated by intermediaries that buffer whole responses; under load events queue and are delivered in batches. The RFC does not describe how to detect or recover lost events. | [RFC 6202](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6202.html) | 1 (informational RFC) | The loss-recovery job therefore belongs to the application protocol (cursor), not to the transport |
| F222 | WebSocket (RFC 6455): Ping/Pong control frames serve as keepalive and liveness check, and a Ping must be answered with a Pong; the Close handshake exists because TCP closure is not always reliable through intermediaries; clients must mask frames sent to a server so intermediaries are not confused; the Origin header protects browser users only and any non-browser client can send any origin string; connections on port 443 succeed more often than on port 80. Section 10 (security considerations) was beyond the text returned: not read. | [RFC 6455](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6455.html) (first 100,000 of 176,684 characters read) | 1 | WebSockets are bidirectional, which the feed does not need (writes use the request/response write path, other parts); a held stream still needs application-level ping and a cursor to resume |
| F223 | NAT behaviour (RFC 5382): a NAT may drop an idle TCP mapping; if it does, the established-connection idle timeout must be at least 2 h 4 min; when a mapping expires the NAT may either send RST or silently drop, so an endpoint can keep a "connected" socket that no longer works. | [RFC 5382](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5382.html) | 1 | The RFC is a requirement on compliant NATs; consumer routers and carrier-grade NAT are not shown to comply. Egyptian ISP behaviour: NOT SOURCEABLE (not searched beyond the RFC; see S201). A silently dead socket is the main reason a client must treat "no event for N seconds" as a missed push |
| F224 | Reverse proxy default behaviour (nginx, the documented example): `proxy_buffering` is on by default, so events can sit in the proxy buffer; `proxy_read_timeout` defaults to 60 s and counts time between reads, so an idle stream longer than that is cut; the upstream can switch buffering off for one response with the `X-Accel-Buffering` header. | [nginx ngx_http_proxy_module](https://nginx.org/en/docs/http/ngx_http_proxy_module.html) | 2 (nginx version not pinned; the Kodeebo proxy is undecided: the stack reference lists Caddy v2.11.7 and Traefik v3.7.14 as candidates, `docs/stack-reference/docker-compose/VERSION.md`) | Caddy and Traefik stream and idle-timeout behaviour: NOT SOURCEABLE - turn budget, not fetched. The point holds for any proxy: heartbeat under the proxy's idle timeout, buffering off for the stream route, and a spike through the real proxy (S202) |
| F225 | NOT SOURCEABLE - Egyptian consumer ISP and mobile-carrier behaviour for long-lived HTTP connections (idle timeouts, CGNAT, transparent proxies, forced resets). Searched: none beyond the RFCs above; no primary source for Egypt was looked for. | - | - | Treat as unknown; design the client to survive a silent dead connection (section 4, C2 options) and measure on the pilot venues' real lines (S202) |

| F308 | **Backward jumps without tampering are documented:** on Windows 10 and later, restarting a PC that has no outbound SSL traffic (closed network) can reset the clock to a date at least one day in the past, because the Secure Time Seeding feature of the Windows Time service uses stale registry data; Microsoft says this "overrides changes that were made by an administrator". Workarounds: resync, reconnect to the internet, or disable via `UtilizeSslTimeData`. | [Computer clock resets to a previous date and time](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/troubleshoot/windows-server/active-directory/client-clock-reverts-to-previous-time) | 2 | Page dated 2026-02-12. A venue that is offline for days is exactly this condition. So a backward wall-clock jump is **an anomaly to record, not proof of fraud**, and a grace design that locks the app on any backward jump would lock honest venues |
| F309 | **Standalone PCs and the Windows Time service:** non-domain PCs are configured by default to sync with `time.windows.com`; Microsoft says accuracy cannot be guaranteed on PCs with intermittent or no network. | F310 | **Oscillator drift bounds for the monotonic clock:** PC crystal oscillators are typically within 30-50 ppm and rarely off by up to 500 ppm; at 50 ppm a 24 h interval is wrong by about 4.3 s; Windows 8+ calibrates frequency offset at start-up. QPC values from different threads within 1 tick have ambiguous ordering; frequency is fixed at boot and may be a fixed 10 MHz under a hypervisor. (QPC counting sleep/hibernate and being independent of time-zone, DST and admin clock changes is already desktop-note F117 and is not re-researched.) | [Acquiring high-resolution time stamps](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/sysinfo/acquiring-high-resolution-time-stamps) | 2 | Page dated 2018-05-31, updated 2025-12-13. Arithmetic: 500 ppm over 7 days is about 302 s - the grace check needs a tolerance that is a config value, not a hardcoded number (computed from the source's ppm figure) |
| F311 | **JWT claims (RFC 7519):** `exp` = rejected at or after the time, with a small clock-skew allowance "typically a few minutes"; `nbf`, `iat` (issue time, so age can be computed), `jti` (unique id, can help block replay), `iss`, `aud` (recipient absent from a present `aud` MUST reject). Section 7.2 validation does not itself check `exp`/`nbf`/`aud`; the application does. All claims are optional. | [RFC 7519](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7519) | 1 | Read 2026-10-16. Relevant fact: the standard's `exp` check compares to the **verifier's wall clock**, which is exactly what a tampered clock defeats - so the lease's expiry must be re-expressed in monotonic terms (D2 design) |

| F326 | **Executed: Egypt 2026 transitions and why local time must not be used for elapsed time.** In `postgres:18.6` (tzdata 2026c) with session zone `Africa/Cairo`: 2026-04-23 22:00 UTC is 2026-04-24 01:00 +03 (local 00:00-01:00 on 24 April does not exist); 2026-10-29 21:00 UTC is 23:00 +02 (local 23:00-24:00 on 29 October occurs twice). A 70-minute interval across the autumn change shows as 10 minutes by local-clock subtraction; a 60-minute interval across the spring change shows as 120 minutes. PostgreSQL resolved the local input `2026-10-29 23:30` to the later (+02) occurrence and the nonexistent `2026-04-24 00:30` to 01:30 +03. `now()` is the transaction start; `clock_timestamp()` advanced past it in the same transaction. A business-day rule of "local time minus 4 hours" put 23:30, 02:30 and 03:30 local on the same business day (the 4 h cut-off is an example, not a requirement). | `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/d-pg-time.sh`, `d-pg-time.sql`, output `d-pg-time.output.txt` (run 2026-10-16, `postgres:18.6`) | E | Confirms F301 and F322 (no conflict). ### 3.5 Part E — fault injection, invariants and oracle, spike design

Tiers per `research-protocol.md`; E = executed evidence. `PENDING — S4xx` = planned, not run.

#### [E] 3.1 E1 — crash, power-loss and kill methods

| # | Claim | Source (link) | Tier | Notes |
|---|-------|---------------|------|-------|
| F401 | SQLite tests crash recovery by **simulation, not real power cuts**. Harness 1: a child process crashes at a random point during a write while a special VFS randomly reorders and corrupts unsynced writes; the parent reopens and checks each change is fully done or fully rolled back using `integrity_check`. Harness 2 (TH3): an in-memory VFS snapshots the filesystem after N I/Os, then applies random power-loss-style damage; each snapshot is tested several times. The page does not describe a torn-sector damage model or give crash-test iteration counts. | [SQLite — How SQLite Is Tested](https://www.sqlite.org/testing.html) | 2 | Fetched 2026-10-16. |
| F402 | SQLite **enumerates the failure point**: a VFS is rigged to fail after N I/Os, N is moved one step later each loop until the operation completes with no injected fault; the same pattern is used for out-of-memory. Faults can be one-shot or persistent, and are stacked (an I/O error during recovery from an earlier crash). | [SQLite — How SQLite Is Tested](https://www.sqlite.org/testing.html) | 2 | Design lesson: exhaustive point enumeration first, randomness on top. |
| F403 | SQLite's own crash-safety rests on stated assumptions: sector writes are not atomic by default; partial writes are "linear" (first or last bytes change); the OS may reorder writes, so SQLite flushes at key points; **flush may lie** (Windows `FlushFileBuffers()` can be disabled via the registry, some drive caches report data stored while still volatile) and SQLite cannot detect it; file delete/truncate is assumed atomic. Hot-journal removal and `synchronous=OFF` break the guarantees. | [SQLite — Atomic Commit](https://www.sqlite.org/atomiccommit.html) | 2 | Complements desktop-note F105/F106. A process kill or a VM power-off cannot exercise a lying drive cache. |
| F404 | Windows `TerminateProcess`: unconditional exit; the process cannot prevent it; all threads stop; pending I/O is requested cancelled; no cleanup runs. | [TerminateProcess (Microsoft Learn)](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/processthreadsapi/nf-processthreadsapi-terminateprocess) | 2 | Page dated 2025-12-30 in its metadata. |
| F405 | Linux `SIGKILL` cannot be caught, blocked or ignored. | [signal(7)](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/signal.7.html) | 2 | |
| F406 | Hyper-V `Stop-VM -TurnOff` is documented as equivalent to disconnecting power from the VM and can lose unsaved data; `-Force` is a guest-assisted shutdown. Doc page lists Windows Server 2016/2019/2022/2025 monikers. Whether the **host** write cache or virtual-disk caching mode makes this stronger or weaker than a real pull NOT SOURCEABLE — not searched (Hyper-V virtual-disk cache docs not fetched). | [Stop-VM (Microsoft Learn)](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/module/hyper-v/stop-vm) | 2 | A guest power-off tests guest OS + SQLite; it does not test the physical drive. |
| F407 | QEMU monitor has `quit`/`q` (quits the emulator), `system_powerdown` (power down "if supported", guest-assisted) and `system_reset`. The page does not say `quit` is equivalent to a power cut. Docs version shown: QEMU 11.1.50 (a development version, not a release). | [QEMU monitor](https://www.qemu.org/docs/master/system/monitor.html) | 2 | Linux-host VM option only; Windows-guest image does not yet exist (caller note). |
| F408 | Linux **dm-log-writes** records block writes in completion order (held until the next flush; writes completing after a flush was issued are excluded, modelling a worst-case power failure), supports named marks, and a userspace `replay-log` replays to a mark and can run an fsck-style check between FUA points. | [dm-log-writes (kernel docs)](https://docs.kernel.org/admin-guide/device-mapper/log-writes.html) | 2 | Linux block-device level; needs a dedicated block device (not stated, inference). Kernel version of the page not shown. |
| F409 | Linux **dm-flakey** makes a device fail on a schedule (up/down intervals): all I/O errors by default, or `error_writes`, **`drop_writes`** (writes silently discarded), `corrupt_bio_byte`, random read/write corruption with a probability. | [dm-flakey (kernel docs)](https://docs.kernel.org/admin-guide/device-mapper/dm-flakey.html) | 2 | `drop_writes` is the closest documented model of a lying write cache on Linux. |

#### [E] 3.4 E2 — runs needed, standards, ledger practice

| F431 | **Rule of three**: after 0 events in n independent trials, the one-sided 95% upper bound on the event probability is about 3/n (from (1−p)^n = 0.05 and ln 0.05 ≈ −3); 99% → 4.6/n, 99.9% → 6.9/n. Credited to Hanley & Lippman-Hand, JAMA 249(13):1743–5, 1983. I could not read the paper text (PDF returned binary), so the citation and the 4.6/6.9 variants rest on a search summary of the paper's page and secondary pages; the maths is re-derived and executed in F432. | [Hanley & Lippman-Hand PDF (McGill)](https://jhanley.biostat.mcgill.ca/c607/ch08/zero_numerator.pdf) | 4 (paper, not read) | The bound covers only the population the trials were drawn from. |
| F432 | Exact zero-failure bound: n ≥ ln α / ln(1−p). For 95%: p=1% → 299 runs, 0.5% → 598, 0.1% → 2,995, 0.01% → 29,956; for 99%: 0.1% → 4,603; for 99.9%: 0.1% → 6,905. Seeing a p=0.1% defect at least once in 1,000 runs has probability 0.632. Rule of three tracks the exact figure within 0.2% at these sizes. | `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/e-runs-bound.py` → `e-runs-bound.out.txt` (python3 -I, host Linux 7.2.9 x86_64, 2026-10-16) | E | Pure arithmetic; no system under test. |

It shows the oracle can fail (mutation self-test idea of Section 5.3) and that `integrity_check` alone would have missed the defect. It says nothing about durability under power loss (process kill keeps the OS cache), the encrypted store, or any real design. | `docs/research/evidence/local-first-sync/e-killprobe.py` → `e-killprobe.out.txt` (`python3 -I e-killprobe.py 20`; SQLite 3.53.4, Python 3.14.7, Linux 7.2.9 x86_64, 2026-10-16) | E | SQLite 3.53.4 is above the WAL-reset fix line 3.51.3 in desktop-note F106 (the probe is single-writer anyway). |
| F441 | Scenarios "client crash between local commit and enqueue", "server crash after commit before ack", "restored backup replaying old sequence numbers", "cloned disk", "partial batch" are derived from the fault-point matrix (Section 5.2), not from a source. External sources for restored-backup/clone-detection designs: NOT SOURCEABLE — not searched; belongs to Parts B/C. | n/a | n/a | Proposal, not finding. |
| F443 | SQLite, errors inside a transaction: `SQLITE_FULL`, `SQLITE_IOERR`, `SQLITE_INTERRUPT` and `SQLITE_NOMEM` "may or may not" roll the transaction back. SQLite tries to undo only the failing statement and keep the earlier statements of the transaction, but may cancel the whole transaction; the application can tell which through `sqlite3_get_autocommit()` / `sqlite3_txn_state()`, and may issue an explicit `ROLLBACK` so the state is always the same (a `ROLLBACK` after an automatic rollback fails harmlessly). | [sqlite.org/lang_transaction.html § 3](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_transaction.html) (fetched 2026-10-16, current docs) | 2 | Consequence for SC-20: after a disk-full error in the "close and pay" transaction the app must `ROLLBACK` explicitly; continuing and committing could keep the bill rows without the outbox row. |
| F444 | `SQLITE_FULL`: a write could not complete because the disk is full, for the main database file **or** temporary files (which may sit on a smaller partition). A full disk normally gives `SQLITE_FULL`, not `SQLITE_IOERR`. | [sqlite.org/rescode.html](https://www.sqlite.org/rescode.html) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 2 | SC-20 injects both codes. |
| F445 | `PRAGMA quick_check` runs in O(N) where `integrity_check` needs O(N log N) (N = total rows); it skips UNIQUE checks and the check that index content matches table content, and is otherwise the same. `integrity_check(N)` stops after N errors (default 100); `integrity_check(TABLENAME)` is a "partial integrity check" of one table and its indexes (since 3.33.0, 2020-08-14); the freelist is verified in a partial check only when TABLENAME is `sqlite_schema`. `quick_check` lists the same argument forms; their meaning is implied, not restated. | [sqlite.org/pragma.html](https://www.sqlite.org/pragma.html) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 2 | Options for SC-24 if the start-up check is too slow. How SQLCipher/SQLite3MC page authentication interacts with a partial check: NOT SOURCEABLE — test. |
| F446 | Locking in rollback-journal mode: any number of SHARED (read) locks may coexist, but writing the database file needs an EXCLUSIVE lock; the writer "might have to wait" until current SHARED locks clear, and its PENDING lock stops new readers meanwhile; the EXCLUSIVE lock is held until commit. WAL mode instead lets readers and a writer proceed concurrently (the page says this is "mostly true"). | [sqlite.org/lockingv3.html §§ 3, 5](https://www.sqlite.org/lockingv3.html); [sqlite.org/wal.html](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html) (fetched 2026-10-16) | 2 | The OQ28 A choice (rollback journal) gives up WAL's reader/writer concurrency: SC-26 measures what the counter feels. |

## 4. Options Considered

### 4.1 Decision areas across the five parts

| # | Decision area | Options considered (tables in 4.3) | Recommended by the part | Rests on (flags for check C6) | Decided by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

### 4.2 Cross-part consistency (merge observations — inference unless an F-number is cited)

1. **The parts agree on one core.** Per-device identity and gap-free sequence, a client-generated command id, tenant-leading keys (F110, F128, F207), append-only money facts corrected by new records (B § 5, C M1, D3-B), and "flag, never block or rewrite" for anomalies (B3-A, C's review list, D3-B). Parts B–D therefore fully specify the custom path (A option I). The PowerSync path needs the same server logic, because PowerSync leaves exactly-once, validation and conflicts to our upload handler (F5, F7, F8).
2. **A vs B on rejections.** PowerSync's upload queue is a blocking FIFO that retries 4xx/5xx indefinitely (F6, F7); B3-A records a permanent rejection and advances. They agree only if the handler records the rejection in the inbox and returns 2xx with error details, as the PowerSync docs advise (F7). To be confirmed in S3.
3. **A vs B on atomicity.** B2-A applies one command per business action atomically; PowerSync uploads row-level PUT/PATCH/DELETE entries (F5), and the pages read do not say whether one local transaction uploads as one unit. One insert-only "command" row carrying the whole action (Part A's inference) would satisfy B2-A — NOT SOURCEABLE until S3.
4. **A vs C on the feed.** Adopting PowerSync replaces C's cursor B with checkpoints over PostgreSQL logical replication (F6, F9). Part C notes that logical decoding retains WAL and is heavy to operate (F205); PowerSync's own operating cost is NOT SOURCEABLE (F33).
5. **Retention ties B, C and D together.** B keeps inbox rows with no timer (B § 5); C needs a tombstone window after which a device must re-bootstrap (F219); D makes grace a per-plan duration (D2-B). Proposed rule for the ADR: tombstone window ≥ longest N5 grace + longest retry delay. A device past the window re-bootstraps the feed, but its outbox is still accepted because the inbox does not expire (OQ4).
6. **Numbering is one question asked twice** (B Q2; C N1 and its first open question). Device code + local daily counter needs no coordination (F211, F212) but cannot give a gap-free branch-wide sequence; whether Egyptian rules require one is NOT SOURCEABLE (OQ5).
7. **"Acknowledged" depends on local durability.** B's probes used WAL with `synchronous=FULL` (F122); desktop-note F106 says `NORMAL` can lose the latest commits on power loss. Proposed for the spike: `synchronous=FULL`, so "acknowledged" means durably committed on the device (E Q1, OQ11).
8. **E's fault point P3 disappears** under B's single local transaction (F122). E's toy probe shows a two-transaction design loses the money event at P3, and that `PRAGMA integrity_check` alone misses it (F442).
9. **Billing determinism is half proven.** The integer function matched byte for byte in four languages (F325), but the server-side SQL port did not match, and that run was not saved, so device–server parity is PENDING S308 (OQ23).
10. **Security IDs are partly looked up.** D and E cite ASVS 5.0.0 and API Top 10 2023 IDs from the official files (F323, F324, F434–F436); A, B and C mark them NOT SOURCEABLE. The merged list in § 6 uses only the looked-up IDs.
