G and H are not assessed.

#### 4.3.2 Part B — write path: idempotency, ordering and atomicity, delivery

Licence note: Kodeebo is hosted only; no option ships Redis, Dragonfly, Mongo, Grafana-family tools or an OTel Collector to a customer, so none is licence-blocked. `docs/ROADMAP.md` does not exist (NOT SOURCEABLE), so L1 to L4 were read only as the caller summarised them. Redis 8.2 and Dragonfly v2.0.2 are pinned for hosted use (`pins.yaml`).

##### [B] B1 Idempotent effects

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability (run, upgrade, self-hosted fit) |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|---------------------------------------------|
| B1-A | Client-generated random `command_id` per business action, stored in the outbox row; server inbox table keyed `(tenant_id, device_id, command_id)` with the payload hash, outcome and stored response; replay returns the stored outcome; same id with another payload is an error (F102, F118, F124, F128). | One table plus one insert per command; about one day to build the apply function on either backend | Low: pure database constraints; risk is retention growth (below) | One indexed insert plus, on replay, one PK lookup; not measured (S105) | Inbox rows double as the audit trail (principle 1); no extra service; `ON CONFLICT` semantics are documented for PG 18 (F105) |
| B1-B | Idempotency-Key HTTP header with a time-limited cache (Stripe style, or the expired IETF draft) in Redis 8.2 or the database, keys pruned after a window (F101, F102, F118). | Header handling plus a cache and expiry job | High for Kodeebo: a device offline for more than 5 days will outlive any 24-hour style window (F118), after which a replay is processed twice; the draft is expired and gives no duration (F101, F102) | Cache hit is fast; cache loss or eviction silently allows duplicates | Extra moving part (Redis or TTL job); a flush or failover loses dedupe state for money |
| B1-C | Natural-key idempotency: upsert business rows by their own keys (bill id, payment id) with no inbox. | Lowest | High: cannot return the original response, cannot tell an altered payload from a replay, and cannot record rejected commands for review | Cheapest | Gaps in the trail for rejected or conflicting commands, which principle 1 forbids |

Header, if wanted at the HTTP edge: a standard name exists only as an expired draft (F101); using the same name with the B1-A semantics is possible, but Kodeebo's own contract in `contracts/` governs.

##### [B] B2 Ordering and atomicity

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| B2-A | Per-device gap-free sequence from a counter row incremented in the same local transaction as the business rows and the outbox row (F111, F121, F122); server keeps a per-device cursor and advances it with one compare-and-set `UPDATE ... WHERE last = seq - 1`; a gap is refused and the device resends from the cursor (F126). One command = one whole business action (session end, lines, payments, ledger entries, stock deductions, receipt number) applied in one PostgreSQL transaction. | Counter row plus cursor row; small | Low to medium: restored backups and clones reuse sequence numbers (F123); the cursor alone cannot prevent that, only detect it (B3) | One extra row update per command, serialised per device only; other devices unaffected; throughput not measured (S103) | Deterministic, easy to explain to support; sequence and cursor are visible to the owner as "sync lag" |
| B2-B | Hybrid logical clock timestamps from the device; server orders by (HLC, device). | Medium: clock logic on every client | Not researched (F129); depends on device wall clocks that N5 treats as untrusted; no gap detection (a missing command is invisible) | Same as A | Harder to debug; no direct "what is missing" answer |
| B2-C | Server arrival order only (no device sequence). | Lowest | High: concurrent retries and parallel connections reorder commands, breaking brief F2 and gap detection; the ordering requirement in the brief fails | Highest | Nothing to monitor; nothing can be proved |
| B2-D | Serializable isolation on every handler instead of a cursor (F104). | Retry loop on 40001 in every handler | Medium: still needs a per-device sequence to know order; serialization failures under contention | Lower under concurrency | Retry logic in each language stack |

Locking variants for B2-A on PostgreSQL 18 (same result, different trade-offs): (i) the compare-and-set `UPDATE` itself (tested, F126); (ii) `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the cursor row first (F106, F109); (iii) `pg_advisory_xact_lock` on a hash of tenant and device (F107, F108), which has a hash-collision case and uses the shared lock memory; (iv) `SKIP LOCKED` queues, which F109 says give an inconsistent view and are not an ordering tool. Option (i) needs no extra construct; (ii) is the choice if the apply step reads before it writes.

Client outbox variants: (a) outbox row in the same local transaction (tested, F122); (b) triggers or change capture over business tables (more moving parts, not tested); (c) a local append-only journal from which tables are derived (largest change to the local model). SQLite constraint: one writer at a time and `SQLITE_BUSY` handling (F111), so the sender thread must keep its transactions short.

##### [B] B3 Delivery and failure handling

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| B3-A | At-least-once delivery in ordered batches (a prefix of the outbox); server returns a per-command result and the new cursor; transient failure stops the batch at that command; a permanent rejection is recorded as `rejected` in the inbox, the cursor advances and later commands proceed (F127); retry with exponential backoff and full jitter, base and cap from config (F117); 401 means "not applied", refresh and resend the same commands (F115); 413 for oversize, 429 and 503 honour `Retry-After` (F115). | Medium: per-command result protocol, a review queue | Medium: a rejected money command is a visible gap between what the counter showed and what the server holds; needs a review workflow and an owner-visible state (brief F3, brief F4) | Batches amortise round trips; sizes unmeasured (S105) | Clear states: pending, sent, acked, rejected; replays are harmless (F124) |
| B3-B | Stop the line: a rejected command blocks all later ones for that device until a human resolves it. | Low | High for principle 2 in effect: the counter works, but the owner's numbers stop moving until someone intervenes, and the backlog grows without bound | Same | Strict order preserved; poison commands become outages |
| B3-C | Skip and continue without advancing the cursor past the rejected command. | Low | High: every later command is a gap, so the cursor design (B2-A) no longer works | Wasteful resends | Needs a second mechanism to carry skipped commands |

Backoff variants: full jitter, equal jitter and decorrelated jitter all reduce work under contention (F117); the formulas were not captured (F130), so the choice and the numbers wait for S105. Restore and clone detection options: (1) compare the incoming command for an already-used `(device, seq)` with the stored one and flag the device on mismatch (F123, F124) - cheap, detects after the fact; (2) add a server-issued, per-registration token stored with the DPAPI-protected key (desktop-note F112 to F115) - not researched here, depends on the key custody design; (3) require the device to present the cursor value the server last acknowledged and refuse a lower value - catches a restored device before it sends. Options (1) and (3) together need no new infrastructure; option (2) is PENDING - S109.

#### 4.3.3 Part C — conflict policy, server-to-device feed, multi-device readiness

Licence: every option below is a design pattern, not a library. No option was found that breaks L1-L4 or T1, and Kodeebo is hosted only. Named products (Replicache, Toast and so on) are evidence of behaviour, were not evaluated for licence or as dependencies, and nothing here is "licence-blocked". Costs are qualitative (team of two); no measured numbers exist (F241).

##### [C] 4.1 C1: conflict policy per data class

| Data class | Option | What can conflict | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Money facts (ledger entries, payments, audit records) | M1: append-only facts minted on the device with a device-scoped id and per-device sequence; server applies in device order and ignores a repeat of the same (tenant, device, sequence); corrections are new compensating entries | Nothing in the facts themselves: inserts are monotonic (F213) and I-confluent (F211). Conflicts are in derived totals (day close) | Low-medium: id scheme and idempotent apply (write-path detail is part B) | A bug in dedup double-counts (the main risk 1 failure); needs fault injection (S205) | One insert per fact | Easy audit: nothing is edited. Matches brief F2 and the per-client mutation id pattern (F217) |
| | M2: state rows (balance fields) merged by last-writer-wins | A late-arriving older write overwrites a newer one; money silently lost | Low | Breaks principle 1; LWW discards one of two concurrent writes by design (the CRDT essay notes CRDTs instead keep both for the application, F215) | Cheap | Hard to audit |
| Session state machine (one open session per station, move station, tier change, backdated start) | S1: sessions as ordered events (start with frozen price snapshot, move, tier change, end) applied in device order; Tier 1 has one writer device per branch so "one open session per station" is checked by that device and re-checked by the server at apply | Uniqueness of a specific value is not I-confluent (F211): two devices could open the same station. With one device it cannot happen; with two it must be prevented or flagged | Medium | Server re-check must flag, not reject, an already-sold session (rejecting loses money); backdated start needs the approver recorded on the event (abuse case 4) | Event rows are small | Billing is recomputed from events plus the frozen price (G12), so a reviewer can replay it |
| | S2: station ownership lease: a device holds a server-granted lease on a station set; only the holder writes that station | Two devices both claiming a station: prevented by the lease, at the cost of a station being unavailable on other devices while the lease is held or the holder is offline | Higher: lease issue, expiry and renewal on the server | Lease expiry needs a time both sides trust, and the device clock is untrusted (F218 lead; desktop note F117) | Negligible | Needed only from the second device on; adds a failure state the cashier sees (brief F4) |
| Bill or tab being edited or voided | B1: lines and voids as appended events on a bill id; the bill total is derived; a dashboard void of a bill the device also edited is applied as a compensating entry and flagged | Dashboard void vs offline line addition; two edits of the same line | Medium | Reviewer must see both; void of a paid bill needs the refund rule (spec) | Small | Same replay model as sessions |
| | B2: whole-bill replace, last writer wins | Silent loss of lines or of a void | Low | Breaks "never lost" | Cheap | Poor |
| Counters and allocations (daily order numbers, receipt numbers) | N1: number = device code + local counter per business day (device-scoped namespace; the vendor example is a register-id prefix, F235); coordination-free (F212) | Uniqueness holds by construction; contiguity across devices does not exist | Low | Gaps or interleaving are visible to the owner; whether Egyptian rules need a gap-free branch sequence is NOT SOURCEABLE (F241) | None | Tier 1 with one device still looks like 1, 2, 3 if the device code is hidden from the display form |
| | N2: server-assigned gap-free sequence | Needs the server for every number; a PostgreSQL sequence is gappy anyway (F201) so gap-free needs a locked counter row | Medium | Violates principle 2 offline (counter waits) | Serialises writes per branch | Simple to explain, wrong for offline |
| | N3: server leases blocks of numbers to the device in advance (reservation) | Block exhausted offline; unused numbers become gaps | Medium | Needs a second source of truth for number blocks | Negligible | Gives contiguous runs per device |
| Stock quantity (the last item sold twice) | K1: stock is a sum of movement facts (sale, void, adjustment, receipt); the device never refuses a sale for stock; a negative result is flagged for review | Two sales of the last item both succeed; the lower bound is non-I-confluent for decrement (F211) | Low | Overselling is accepted by design; owner sees the flag | Sum or maintained counter on server | Honest and auditable; vendors also do not update stock offline (F233 lead, F234) |
| | K2: bounded counter / escrow: each device holds rights to sell N units; rights move only via the server (F214) | Device out of rights offline must refuse the sale or break the bound | High: rights allocation and transfer | Devices cannot transfer to each other, so spare rights on one device are stranded; blocks selling, against principle 2 | Small | Worth it only for scarce, high-value items; not Tier 1 |
| Owner-edited configuration (prices, settings, reason lists, permissions) | C-1: immutable versioned config snapshots; the server is the only writer; each device fact carries the config version it used; open sessions use the price frozen at start (G12); dashboard edits use If-Match on the version (412, F226) | Dashboard edit vs another dashboard edit (server rejects the stale one); config version used by an offline device older than current (flagged, not rejected) | Medium | Permission revocation takes effect only after the device syncs (abuse case 3); N5 rides the same feed | One snapshot per change | A reviewer can see which rules a sale ran under |
| | C-2: per-field last-writer-wins including device-side config writes | Device-side writes to config would exist at all | Low | Violates principle 4 (owner sets, counter follows) | Cheap | Poor |
| Whole-model alternatives | CRDT for everything | Same-property edits are kept as conflicts for the application anyway (F215); counters cannot enforce bounds without escrow (F214); primary CRDT text not read (F216) | High | Unbounded history (F215); a second data model beside PostgreSQL | History grows | Hard for 2 engineers |
| | Operation-based rebase with server reconciliation (F217) | Server result may differ from the local speculative result | Medium: replay of pending operations on the client | Operations must be deterministic and versioned | Replays on each pull | The pattern that fits brief F2 and C-1; engine choice is part A |

Human review (all classes): a business day closed offline whose server totals differ from the device totals is flagged (brief F3); negative stock; a session or bill touched by both device and dashboard; a fact carrying an older config version than the server's at apply time; a backdated start (approver and amount); a device whose sequence has a gap.

##### [C] 4.2 C2: server-to-device changes

| Area | Option | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cursor | A: cursor on a serial id (`id > last`) | Lowest | Skips rows committed out of order; shown in the probe (F208, F201); not acceptable | Cheapest | Silent data loss, no signal |
| | B: stamp each change row with `pg_current_xact_id()` (xid8) and serve only rows below the xmin of a snapshot; cursor is the previous horizon (F202, F208) | Low-medium | Any long transaction in the database delays the feed for everyone (F208); needs a timeout policy | One range scan per pull | Needs an alert on horizon age |
| | C: per-tenant change-log sequence allocated under a lock on a tenant counter row held until commit, so sequence order equals commit order (design inference, not sourced; PENDING S204) | Medium | Serialises changes per tenant; deadlock and contention design | Low volume per tenant | Simple cursor for clients; gaps from rollback must be tested |
| | D: logical decoding with a slot feeding a fan-out service (F205) | High | Slot retains WAL if the consumer stops; duplicates after crash; one consumer per slot; failover handling | Lowest query load | Heavy operations for 2 engineers; part A may cover |
| Bootstrap | E: full tenant/branch snapshot read in one Repeatable Read transaction, recording the horizon, then continue from it (F204) | Medium | Large for big catalogues; consistent by construction | One big read | Same path serves new device, local data loss and "cursor too old" |
| | F: only incremental from cursor zero | Low | Needs all tombstones forever | Unbounded | Not viable |
| Deletes | G: tombstones kept for a retention window; a device offline longer than the window must re-bootstrap (F219 shows the resurrection failure otherwise) | Low | Window length is a number to choose (owner: cto) | Small | Server answers "cursor too old" with a 410-style problem (F226) |
| Versioning | H: client sends protocol and schema version; server supports the current and previous; an unsupported client keeps selling and queuing but stops applying the feed and shows "update required"; local schema version in `user_version` (F227); additive changes between majors (F228 pattern) | Medium | Outbox facts live forever, so every server must read every old fact format | None | Update path is part of desktop part 4 |
| | I: single mandatory-upgrade version, server rejects old clients | Low | A venue that cannot update stops selling: violates principle 2 | None | Simple |
| Push | J: plain polling at an interval, with cursor | Lowest | Latency equals interval (price edits wait) | Constant small load | Always correct; works through any proxy |
| | K: SSE stream as a nudge, then pull by cursor; heartbeat under the proxy idle timeout; stream route unbuffered (F220, F224) | Low-medium | Stream may die silently (F223); needs client-side reconnect logic outside a browser (F220) | One held connection per device | Proxy settings matter (F224); spike S202 |
| | L: WebSocket as a nudge, then pull | Medium | Bidirectional capability unused; ping/pong needed (F222) | One held connection | Same as K plus upgrade handling |
| | M: long polling then pull | Low | Held request limits and timeouts (F221) | One request per cycle | Works with simple proxies |

##### [C] 4.3 C3: Tier 1 decisions that would block several devices per branch later

| # | Tier 1 decision to make now | If made the easy way | Multi-device-ready form (recommended) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Device identity | No device id; "the branch" is the writer | Enrolled device id per installation, server-issued, in every fact and every sync request; revocable |
| 2 | Outbox sequence | One sequence per branch | Sequence per (tenant, device) (F217 pattern); dedup key includes device |
| 3 | Numbering | Branch counter | Device code + local daily counter, display form hides the code (N1, F212, F235) |
| 4 | Station ownership | Implicit: the only device owns everything | Explicit owner field per station and per open bill, today always "this device"; server flags a second owner (S2 option when needed) |
| 5 | Shift and drawer scope | Shift per branch | Shift and cash count keyed by drawer and device within a branch; branch-level day aggregates them (a per-POS shift claim exists only in a community thread, tier 5, lead only; no vendor page read states shift scope for several devices) |
| 6 | Stock | A local "quantity on hand" the device edits | Stock derived from movement facts (K1); no device-edited quantity |
| 7 | Who may close the business day | The device closes the day | Day close is a server-side branch event accepted when every enrolled device has drained its outbox up to a stated sequence, or an owner/approver forces it with a flag (day close is non-monotonic, F213, so it needs a single decider) |
| 8 | Feed cursor and config version | One cursor for the branch | Cursor and last-applied config version stored per device; facts stamped with config version |
| 9 | Ordering | Device clock decides order | Order within a device by sequence; across devices by server apply order, never by device clock (time part) |
| 10 | Audit actor | Staff id only | Staff id + device id on every record |

Offline coordination when devices cannot reach each other: none exists in the chosen model (LAN sync is ruled out). Each device works only from its own data; shared things (station state, open bills, stock, numbers) are partitioned (station and tab ownership, device-scoped numbers, stock as facts) so that disagreement is detected and flagged when the facts arrive, not prevented. Published vendors degrade the same way without a LAN hub: orders stay on the device that took them and other devices do not see them (F230, F234; F233 lead); the one vendor feature that avoids it is a LAN hub (Toast, F230), which Kodeebo has ruled out. No vendor documents resolving a double sale of the last item or duplicate numbers (F236).

#### 4.3.4 Part D — session time, N5 trust protocol, clock skew

Bundle licence rules (L1-L4) do not apply: Kodeebo is hosted only; no option below ships a restricted component, so none is "licence-blocked". Statements marked "inferred" are my reasoning from the cited findings, not sourced facts, and rest on spikes that have not run.

##### [D] 4.1 D1 - measuring billable session time

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| D1-A | **Wall clock only:** elapsed = end wall time minus start wall time (UTC or local). | Lowest | Defeated by any clock edit; backward jumps and clock steps happen on honest PCs (F308, F309); local-time subtraction is wrong by an hour on DST days (F326). Fails brief risk 1 | Negligible | Simplest; indefensible in a dispute |
| D1-B | **Anchored monotonic + wall checkpoints (recommended).** At session start persist: session id, start instant as UTC claim, QPC reading and frequency (desktop-note F117, F310), app-run id, boot marker (best available, may be "unknown", F316-F317), the frozen price snapshot (G12) and the rule parameters. While the app runs, elapsed = difference of the monotonic reading, which counts sleep and ignores clock edits, DST and zone changes (desktop-note F117). A checkpoint {UTC, QPC, boot marker, run id} is written every N seconds (config). After a reboot or crash, elapsed since the last checkpoint comes from the wall clock, is tagged `elapsed_source=wall_after_restart`, and raises a flag if the wall clock went backward. Every session segment stores its `elapsed_source`. Billing is the pure integer function of segments (F325). Business-day and zone logic use UTC instants plus the venue's IANA zone resolved with the server's tzdata (F301, F305, F326). | Medium: a Windows clock call per runtime (FFI), a checkpoint writer, the segment model | Wall clock is trusted across a reboot (a tampered clock there under- or over-charges that gap; flagged, not prevented); boot marker unreliable under Fast Startup (F316-F317); a hard power-off loses up to one checkpoint interval | Negligible CPU; checkpoint write on an HDD unmeasured (S309) | Same pure function on device and server, so a mismatch is a bug signal, not a debate |
| D1-C | **D1-B plus opportunistic server time stamps:** whenever online, each API response carries server time and the client stores (server time, QPC, wall) triples, giving later estimates of wall-clock error for the offline stretch. | Medium-high: more protocol and storage | More states to test; estimate quality unmeasured | Negligible | Improves after-the-fact review of D1-B's reboot gap; not needed for the first release |

| Billing-arithmetic option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D1-I | **Integer milliseconds and integer piasters per hour, one half-up rounding at the end; minimum minutes, increment and mode are config.** | Low | Rounding rule choices are business decisions (Q4) | Microseconds (not benchmarked) | Identical in Python, Go, Java, Node (F325) |
| D1-F | Floating point (rate per minute x minutes). | Low | 137 of 5,007 vectors differed from the integer result (F325) | Negligible | Device and server can disagree: a money-trail defect |
| D1-S | Round each segment separately, then add. | Low | Differs from D1-I by up to a piaster per extra segment (inferred); which one owners expect is unknown | Negligible | Needs the idea-pool S3/N4 rule decided (Q4) |

##### [D] 4.2 D2 - N5 lease and trust protocol

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| D2-A | **Opaque "OK" from the server, wall-clock expiry on the device.** | Lowest | Forgeable by a local proxy or hosts-file; a wall-clock expiry is what a clock edit defeats (F311, F318) | None | Rejected |
| D2-B | **Signed lease (JWS compact, EdDSA/Ed25519) with server-time-anchored budget (recommended).** Claims: `iss`, `aud` (this product and device), `sub` (tenant), `jti`, a lease sequence number, `iat` = server time, `status` (active/suspended), `grace_seconds` for the plan (a duration, not a date), `kid`, `typ` set to a lease-specific value (F311-F315). On receipt the device records QPC + wall; remaining grace = `grace_seconds` minus time consumed, where consumed time uses the monotonic difference within a boot and the persisted high-water mark across reboots. The standard `exp` check is not the control (it uses the verifier's wall clock, F311). On expiry the app enters "must reach the server" (behaviour: Q2). Public keys (current and next) are embedded in the code-signed app (the update channel is covered in `desktop-client-technology.md` part 4) and selected by `kid`; no `jku`/`x5u` (F315, ASVS V9.1.3). The device keeps the highest lease sequence seen and rejects lower ones; the server records which sequence each sync used. | Medium: server signer + key custody (ASVS V13.3.1, V13.3.4), client verifier, small clock state | A power-off with the RTC set back loses the unknown gap unless a penalty policy applies (see D2-P). A local administrator can restore a disk snapshot or patch the app (section 5) | Verify once per lease load; negligible | Lease renewal rides the normal sync; key rotation = ship next key in an update before it is used |
| D2-C | **Hardware-bound licence (TPM attestation).** | High | TPM present only on some venue PCs (desktop-note F114: presence unknown); two code paths | Hardware call at renewal | Not recommended for the first release; possible later upgrade |
| D2-L | **Longer-lived tokens, no monotonic accounting (plain JWT with `exp`).** | Low | Clock edit extends grace; fails brief risk 8 | None | Rejected |

| Backward-at-boot policy (D2-P) | Description | Cost | Risk | Operability |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D2-P1 | If the wall clock at start is earlier than the stored high-water mark by more than a tolerance, charge a configured penalty of grace (default set in `/license-design`) and raise a flag; if it is later, charge the wall difference. | Low | Honest venues hit by the Windows clock reset after offline restarts (F308) pay the penalty | Penalty value is a licence parameter |
| D2-P2 | Treat any backward-at-boot as "grace used up": the app must reach the server. | Low | Locks honest offline venues after the F308 case; strongest against rollback | Needs an unlock path (Q3) |
| D2-P3 | Ignore backward jumps (use only the high-water mark). | Lowest | **A powered-off PC with the hardware clock set back does not consume grace for that gap** (inferred from D1-B/D2-B design; S306 to prove) - fails brief risk 8 | None |

##### [D] 4.3 D3 - clock skew at sync

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| D3-A | Store only the device timestamp. | Lowest | Server cannot detect or explain any clock problem; day-close flag brief F3 has nothing to compare | None | Rejected |
| D3-B | **Store both on every event, flag and never block (recommended).** Each event carries: `device_claim_utc`, device monotonic reading with frequency, run id, boot marker (may be unknown), device sequence number, `elapsed_source`; the server adds `server_received_at` taken at the API edge with a statement-level clock (not the transaction start, F326) and a per-batch `device_sent_claim_utc`. Derived values are stored in separate columns tagged with the method: `skew_estimate = server_received_at - device_sent_claim_utc` (includes network delay), per-event position inside the batch from the sequence number. Flags (all thresholds are config, no number is proposed because none could be sourced): large skew, backward step between consecutive sequence numbers, wall vs monotonic divergence beyond drift bound (F310), boot-marker change, lease-sequence regress, elapsed taken from wall after restart, event outside the open business day. Flags attach to the event, to the session and to the day-close summary (brief F3); an audit event (P6) records both times. Nothing is rejected or rewritten; money records are never mutated (principle 1). | Medium | Threshold values need real data; too many flags train the owner to ignore them | One extra write per batch; negligible | Review queue for the owner; support can reconstruct from both clocks |
| D3-C | **Correct the device timestamps on the server** (replace claims with `server_received_at - offset`). | Medium | Destroys the evidence of the claim; the offset estimate is wrong for any stretch spent offline; violates "a correction is a new record" | Negligible | Rejected as the stored value; allowed only as a derived column (D3-B) |
| D3-D | **Reject events whose skew is above a limit.** | Low | Blocks the counter or loses sales; contradicts principle 2 | Negligible | Rejected |

Staff clock in/out (W6): D3-B applies unchanged; the additional question is retention and access (Q11). The time of a named employee's clock in/out is personal data (classification is a decision for counsel, F329; **closed by founder statement (2026-10-18)**); do not copy it to telemetry.

#### 4.3.5 Part E — fault injection, invariants and oracle, spike design

##### [E] 4.1 Network faults (E1)

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability (run, upgrade, self-hosted fit) |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|---------------------------------------------|
| A. Toxiproxy v2.13.0 | TCP proxy between client and server; toxics cut/slow/reset/limit bytes per connection and per direction (direction attachment to be verified) | Free; MIT | Cannot create IP-level duplicates/reorders (TCP hides them); API must stay on loopback (F411) | Proxy overhead not measured: NOT SOURCEABLE | Same Go binary on Windows and Linux; HTTP API scriptable from any language; Docker image; hosted-only product so no bundle impact. Licence: MIT, no restriction |
| B. tc netem (Linux) | Kernel qdisc: delay, loss, duplication, corruption, reordering, rate | Free (part of iproute2); licence not read: NOT SOURCEABLE | Root needed (not sourced); duplicates/reorders visible only below TCP; Linux only | Kernel-level, negligible (inference) | Good in CI and Docker on the dev host; not usable on the Windows reference PC |
| C. clumsy / WinDivert (Windows) | User-mode packet capture/drop/duplicate/reorder via a kernel driver | Free; clumsy MIT; WinDivert LGPLv3/GPLv2 (test-only, not shipped, so no bundle impact) | Win 10/11 support not stated by sources; kernel driver on the test machine; driver-signing status unverified; interactive GUI tool | Unknown | Manual / GUI; scripting not verified. Optional spot-check on the Windows reference PC (`PENDING — S410`) |
| D. In-process fault injection (wrapper around HTTP client/server, failpoints) | Test seams that drop request, drop response after server handler returns, delay, duplicate a call, kill at a named point | Engineer time in each language; libraries exist for Go (pingcap/failpoint, F428) | Tests the code, not the OS/network stack; seams must not leak into production builds | Fast, deterministic | The only method that can hit an exact point such as "after server commit, before the response is written". Language-specific, so the harness needs an adapter per stack |
| E. Windows or Linux firewall rule / interface disable | OS-level block of the server address | Free | Cuts everything; no partial/duplicate faults; sources not fetched | n/a | Coarse "cable pulled" only |

##### [E] 4.2 Crash and power-loss (E1)

| Option | Description | Cost | Risk | Performance | Operability |
|--------|-------------|------|------|-------------|-------------|
| A. Process kill at a chosen point (`SIGKILL` / `TerminateProcess`) | Kill the client or server process at an enumerated failpoint | Free | OS keeps its page cache on a process kill, so unsynced writes survive; cannot show durability loss (inference from F403/F404/F405) | Fast; thousands of runs/hour plausible (unmeasured) | Works on both OSes; the main tool for the matrix |
| B. SQLite-style simulated VFS (F401/F402) | Fault-injecting VFS that reorders, drops and tears unsynced writes | Needs a custom VFS in the SQLite build used; not available in every wrapper (not sourced) | Tests SQLite's own logic, which SQLite already does itself; our risk is the application's transaction boundaries, not SQLite's journal | Fast | Likely duplicates what SQLite already proves. Low priority |
| C. Linux dm-flakey / dm-log-writes (F408/F409) | Block-device level drop or replay of writes up to a mark | Free; Linux root + a spare block device/loop device (not sourced) | Linux only; tests ext4/xfs behaviour, not NTFS on Windows | Slower setup | Only way found to emulate a lying write cache and replay-to-mark. Useful once on the dev host |
| D. VM hard power-off (Hyper-V `Stop-VM -TurnOff`; QEMU `quit`) | Guest loses power instantly | Free; needs a Windows 10/11 image (not yet available) | Guest-level only; host cache effects not sourced (F406); physical drive lies untested | Slow: minutes per run (unmeasured) | Windows runs are possible only after the Windows VM exists (founder, desktop spike phase 0) |
| E. Real power pull on the 4 GB / HDD reference PC | Pull the plug mid-close | Free in money, high in time and wear | Few runs only (see F432: 20 clean runs bound p only to 14%); risks drive/OS damage | n/a | Needed once to catch what VMs cannot; manual |

No DST in the spike; deterministic seeds + enumerated failpoints in a bespoke harness | Seeded RNG for the action and fault schedule; replay by seed + commit (F421 practice) | Engineer time | Real-time wall-clock races are not deterministic; some failures need many reruns | Real time | Smallest; stays language-neutral |
| B. Language-level virtual time (Go `synctest`, F422) | Fake clock for retry/backoff/clock logic inside the Go process | Free; Go-only | Excludes real sockets/syscalls; Kotlin counterpart not sourced | Fast | Good for unit-level clock tests if backend is Go |

Antithesis | Hosted deterministic simulation of containers with network/node/clock faults (F418/F419) | Price NOT SOURCEABLE; commercial | Vendor contact needed; Linux containers only (inference); sends our code to a vendor: privacy/IP review (needs counsel? not legal advice; **closed by founder statement (2026-10-18)**) | Reproducible by design | Fits the backend + headless sync core, not the Windows app. Revisit after the protocol exists |
