| OQ28 | **WAL silent loss (spike finding P1-F1, 2026-10-16; `prototypes/local-first-sync-spike/README.md`).** One flipped byte in the WAL (header or any frame) made the store open normally, pass `integrity_check` and silently hold fewer acknowledged sales (0 or 4 of 5). This held under both SQLite3MC ciphers. The cause is SQLite's cumulative WAL checksum: a damaged frame invalidates it and every later frame ([file format § 4](https://www.sqlite.org/fileformat2.html), tier 2). This contradicts the note's assumption that WAL with `synchronous=FULL` keeps acknowledged sales (4.2 item 7; desktop-note F106). The four options are not mutually exclusive: **(A)** rollback-journal mode, so committed data always sits in the HMAC-checked main file — still to test: a damaged hot journal after a crash mid-transaction, and commit latency on the HDD reference PC; **(B)** keep WAL and add a commit high-water mark outside the database, fsynced and MAC'd with the key, so loss is detected and refused at start, but not prevented; **(C)** checkpoint after every transaction, which shrinks the exposure to a crash window but does not close it; **(D)** sync promptly, so the server's sequence check catches the loss of synced sales only. Phase 1 stays failed until one of these is re-tested. **Spike decision (founder, 2026-10-16): A + B, with D in every case.** The re-run adds tamper cases for a damaged hot journal and for the marker itself. This is a spike decision; the storage ADR still decides the product. **Store-design rule (founder, 2026-10-19, desktop finding G5-F4):** before opening the store, if a hot journal (or a WAL) exists, copy the store, the journal and the commit marker aside first, and delete the copy only after verification passes. SQLite plays a hot journal back inside the open call, so a damaged journal overwrites the store before the app can check it: the desktop spike saw this on Windows in both runtimes (round 4 G5.3, commit `4dfc169`), where the app copied the store only after open and so kept the already-overwritten file. The sync spike's client has done this since phase 1 (P1-F3); the Linux tamper row `hot_journal_page1_flip` (phase 3) damages page 1's image in the hot journal of a migration killed mid-DDL and passes under both ciphers: refused and recorded, SQLite rewrote the file, the pre-open originals kept byte-identical. S206 cell `f_g5f4_hot_journal_page1` then re-bootstraps from that state with the loss reported. **Windows, desktop build 1.0.4 (commit `fa12065`, `TS03J … v1.0.4` lines in `prototypes/desktop-client-spike/evidence/g5/run-log-g5-20261019.txt`): PASS in both runtimes**: with no flip the copy is made, SQLite rolls back, the store verifies and the copy is removed; with 64 bytes flipped SQLite still overwrites the store at open and the app refuses, but the pre-open store and the flipped journal survive byte-identical in the copy, which the `store_open_failed` event names. **Limit, part of the rule:** a failed replay leaves the journal on disk, so every later start would copy again, now the already overwritten store; keep the first copy, one copy per distinct file hash (desktop G5-F5 found the same for refused starts; the sync client shows it on Linux too, finding P3-F17, `evidence/phase3-probe-repeat-copy.out.txt`) | SP: phase 1 re-run; B: storage ADR and desktop storage choice | user (founder), with `data-lead` | decided for the spike; storage ADR open |
| OQ27 | Rollback and renumbering blind spot (TS-01b, TS-02b): an insider can erase offline sales that never reached the server. **Not decided (founder, 2026-10-16).** Candidates M1 file access control (primary), M2 offline-period alerts, M3 shift cash reconciliation and M4 anti-rollback marker (research only) are recorded in 9.1.1, with spike checks TS-08–TS-10. Decided in the sync `/threat-model`, where the founder accepts by name whatever residual risk remains after M1–M3. M1 implies a separate storage process (desktop E2/E3), which affects the desktop process-model decision. **Founder position (2026-10-16), not yet the formal acceptance:** the founder intends to accept the residual risk (an administrator erasing offline sales before they sync) on three conditions. (1) The Windows administrator on a venue PC is only the owner or Kodelix's installer; a cashier is never an administrator (install checklist). (2) The owner sees every offline period (M2). (3) M4 stays a research candidate. The formal acceptance by name is made in the sync `/threat-model`.<br>**Founder input (2026-10-18), not a decision yet:** for a device stolen or burned while offline:<br>(1) sync on any short connection, including a phone hotspot (already tested: SC-25a drains a backlog over short cut windows);<br>(2) an offline-duration alert to the cashier and the owner after a threshold (for example 6 hours; the value is set in the spec);<br>(3) an optional owner setting: an encrypted copy of the store to a removable USB drive every hour, taken home at shift end. Restoring it on a new PC goes through the OQ7 quarantine and review queue (SC-13);<br>(4) the End Day summary printed on paper.<br>**What the spike can test (TS-01b write-up):** (1) done in SC-25a. (2) Testable with injected clocks: the owner alert must come from the server's clock, because a stolen device never reconnects, and the cashier's warning from the device's monotonic time. (3) Testable: the hourly copy, the loss of the device, and the restore on a new PC (new machine id) through quarantine and the owner's choice. That measures what is recovered and what is lost: sales after the last copy that never synced. Restoring needs the store key on the new PC. Key escrow is the key-custody question (desktop-note S11, licensing note), and the spike uses its test key. (4) Not testable in the spike: printing is the desktop spike's real-printer day (S50). The data the paper would carry is SC-31's End Day totals, already checked against the server.<br>**Measured (2026-10-19, tier E, spike README TS-01b leg):** (2) one owner alert per offline episode once the device is silent past the threshold on the server's clock, so it fires while a stolen device stays silent. The cashier's warning comes from the device's monotonic clock. (3) the hourly copy restored on a new PC goes through quarantine (`second_machine`) and the owner's choice. Everything synced and everything in the copy is recovered, the old PC's sales synced after the copy go to the review queue, and exactly the sales after the copy that never synced are lost. | B: sync threat model; desktop process-model ADR | user + `security-lead` | at the sync `/threat-model` |
| OQ29 | **Device retention window and server backup window (founder rule, 2026-10-16; § 9.1.2).** Outbox rows are deleted once a fully parsed ack arrives. Business records stay on the device for a bounded window longer than both ETA's 30-day late-submission route (ETA note F317) and the server's backup/PITR window, and are pruned only after the server confirms its archive. **Provisional values (founder, 2026-10-16; the storage ADR confirms them):** (1) The device keeps business records for **60 days**. (2) The server backup/PITR window must be **shorter than 60 days**. **Set (founder, 2026-10-18; ADR-0005 Decision 6): 30 days**, below the 60-day device retention. (3) "The server confirms its archive" means the server reports a **durable watermark**: the highest sequence contained in a completed backup whose restore has been verified. The device prunes a record only if it is **below that watermark and older than 60 days**. SC-19 and SC-24 use these values. Consequence to test in SC-19 (inference): a restore from any backup inside the server window loses nothing the device cannot resend; only a backup older than 60 days can leave an unrecoverable range. Still open: the store size at 60 days for SC-24 (needs OQ16). The server window is closed: 30 days (founder, 2026-10-18). Assumed, to confirm: ETA's 540-day return window (ETA note F311, F319) is served from the server, not the device | B: storage ADR, backup runbook; SP: SC-19 and SC-24 use the provisional values | user (founder; device values provisional, server window set to 30 days 2026-10-18) | server window closed (30 days, ADR-0005 Decision 6); device values at the storage ADR |
| OQ30 | **Near-real-time delivery of dashboard changes to an online counter (founder input, 2026-10-19).** A change the owner makes on the dashboard should reach an online counter in near real time. To evaluate: a server-to-device "something changed" signal over SSE or WebSocket (options K and L in 4.3.3; F220, F222–F224) that only **triggers the normal cursor pull**. No data ever travels over the signal channel, and a lost signal is covered by the next scheduled pull, so the cursor feed stays the only path for data. Also to evaluate: the nginx settings for long-lived connections (buffering off on the stream route, read and send timeouts above the heartbeat interval, the upgrade headers for WebSocket, worker connection limits), and the connection count on the 8 GB VPS (one held connection per online device plus the dashboards; file-descriptor and memory cost per connection; how a push relay reaches the stream when transaction-pooled PgBouncer cannot carry `LISTEN`, F206/F207). **The mechanism is shared with the dashboard real-time ADR**: one signal channel and one relay, decided once. Spike leg S202 (push through the real proxy and a real venue line) supplies the measurements | B: sync ADR (C2 push) and the dashboard real-time ADR | `cto`, with `infra-engineer` (nginx, VPS sizing) and `offline-sync-specialist` | at the sync ADR, after the phase 3 gate report |

### 9.1 Spike plan — `/prototype` local-first sync spike (phases 0–2 PASS, 2026-10-16; phase 3 gate PASS, 2026-10-19; results in `prototypes/local-first-sync-spike/README.md`)

Directory: `prototypes/local-first-sync-spike/`. Prototype rules apply: throwaway, never imported by production, 127.0.0.1 only, test keys only, shortcuts listed. Part E's full design is in 5.1.5: invariants I1–I9, fault-point matrix P1–P12, run counts, harness contract, scenarios SC-01–SC-18, pass/fail rules, what the spike must not conclude, and evidence to retain. The table below folds every other part's spikes into Part E's phases.

**System under test**: the custom protocol of § 5 — a single local transaction with `synchronous=FULL`; command id + per-device sequence; server inbox + compare-and-set cursor on PostgreSQL 18.6; ordered batches with reject-record-advance; integer billing. **Shortcuts the README must list:** single tenant, no authentication, no rate limits, minimal error UX. Idempotency is not a shortcut; it is the thing under test.

| Phase | Question | Folded-in spikes | Pass signal | Proposed timebox (Part E) |

| 1 | Is "bill closed + outbox row" atomic on the **encrypted** store under process kill at P1–P4 and P10–P12? | S102 (SQLCipher or SQLite3MC, Linux first), S6; tamper TS-03a, TS-04a, TS-05a (9.1.1) | 0 violations over every cell × ≥ 50 seeds; `integrity_check` ok; tamper pass signals in 9.1.1 | ≈ 1–2 days |

The FSL counsel reading (OQ2) is **closed by founder statement (2026-10-18)** | Part A's pass conditions | short, after the phase 3 gate |

**Order to the sync ADR (founder, 2026-10-18).** The sync spike is closed **before the tenancy review and the backend research start**. It runs **in parallel with the desktop spike**; neither has priority over the other. Everything in phases 3, 4 and 5 runs before the sync ADR, except S308 (parked below), in this order:
1. the phase 3 gate (≥ 3,000 runs, with phase 2 rerun at full size in the same campaign) — **PASS, 2026-10-19**;
2. ~~the PowerSync track's S2–S4 on Linux~~ — **closed, not run: PowerSync rejected by ADR-0001 (founder, 2026-10-19)**;
3. phase 4 on the Windows VM (S1 lapsed with the PowerSync track);
4. phase 5.

**Shared Windows VM.** The desktop spike's round 4 also needs the VM. Each spike claims it in the checkpoint (`production/session-state/active.md`) before use and releases it there afterwards. When the desktop spike's block says FREE, the VM goes to whichever spike is ready; neither has priority (founder, 2026-10-18). `clean-ssh2` is restored after each run. A tamper leg both spikes need runs **once** on the shared store, and both spikes cite that run. **Agreed split (sync and desktop spikes, 2026-10-18):**
- **TS-05b:** the desktop spike runs it once in its round 4 (gate G5) through each runtime's binding, against this note's pass signal. Sync phase 4 cites that run.
- **TS-03c and TS-06c:** the desktop spike runs the Windows store half once in round 4: detect, refuse, never write over, keep the damaged file aside, and record Windows's delete-while-open behaviour. The server half (recovery from the server, gap report, re-bootstrap alert) does not depend on the OS. It runs on Linux in phase 3 (TS-03b, TS-06a/b), and the two halves cite each other.
- **Evidence:** `prototypes/desktop-client-spike/evidence/g5/`, one file per leg and runtime, cited here by path and commit once round 4 is done.
- **Stay with sync phase 4:** TS-01c, TS-04b, TS-07b and TS-08. TS-08 takes the desktop spike's round 3 per-user uninstall results (G4.3) as an input.

**Phase 5 limits.** Phase 5 runs **without S308** (server billing parity). S308 is **PARKED. Trigger: the backend-track decision.** It does not hold up the spike's close or the sync ADR. Phase 5's tax order uses a configurable setting, inclusive or exclusive, until ETA answers (OQ8 rule 5).

**Not in this spike (other owners):** S201 cursor under load and S204 tenant-counter cursor (`cto`); S202 push through the real proxy and a venue line (`infra-engineer`, after OQ19); S203 two offline devices per branch (risk 9, after Tier 1); S105 batch size and backoff on a venue link; S106 RLS policy matrix (`multitenancy-specialist`); S108 inbox growth; S307 JWS interoperability.

**Run count N (Part E, F432):** zero violations in 3,000 runs bounds the per-run defect rate below 0.1% at 95% confidence; 30,000 runs bounds it below 0.01%. The bound covers only fault classes the generator reaches; narrow windows are covered by enumerated failpoints, not by chance.

**Packaging conflict (F438) — resolved 2026-10-16:** `/prototype` caps a concept prototype at 1 day and spike mode at about 4 hours, while brief risk 1 asks for 1–2 weeks. The founder chose P2 with a written waiver of the cap (§ 5): one prototype, with each phase gated pass/fail and stopping on the first failure. The 3-PIVOT rule of `/prototype` still applies.

#### 9.1.1 Tamper scenarios (founder, 2026-10-16)

**Principle:** tampering may fail an operation, but it must never lose or double-count money silently, and the owner must see it — "every pound leaves a trail" (principle 1). The spike checks this as one more invariant beside Part E's I1–I9:

- **I10 — tamper is visible.** Every tamper scenario ends in one of two ways: the operation fails safely, or the server records a flagged event that reaches the owner (an alert, plus the day-close review, brief F3). No money or session-time event appears, disappears or changes without such a record.

**Already planned, kept unchanged:** clock set back or forward while running, while powered off and across restart (S301–S306); network cut, slow, lossy, flapping, cut mid-close and duplicate resend (Part E, SC-01–SC-16).

**Where each runs:** "Linux" legs run on the dev host in the phase named, against the encrypted store (SQLCipher or SQLite3MC) and PostgreSQL 18.6. "Windows" legs need the Windows 10/11 VM or the reference PC in phase 4; they share the desktop spike's S10, S11 and S304 machines (desktop note, spike plan). **Mechanism** cites the design element the pass depends on. **Feeds** names the threat model each scenario goes to: *sync* = `docs/architecture/threat-models/local-first-sync.md`; *N5/time* = `docs/architecture/threat-models/n5-lease-and-session-time.md`.

| ID | Scenario | Pass signal | Mechanism under test | Legs | Feeds |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| TS-01 | **Rollback attack.** Copy the local database (no key needed, only file access), make sales offline, restore the old copy, reconnect. Three variants: **(a)** the sales made after the copy were synced before the restore, so the restored device reuses sequence numbers the server already holds; **(b)** the sales made after the copy never reached the server before the restore; **(c)** = (a) and (b) on Windows, with any anti-rollback marker kept outside the database. | The server detects the device sequence going backwards, or a reused command id / used sequence number with different content; it flags the device and alerts the owner; no sale disappears silently. | (a): used-sequence comparison and "refuse a lower cursor" — B3 detection options (1) + (3), F123, F124. **(b): no mechanism in the current design** — the server's cursor equals the copy, so nothing goes backwards from its view and the erased sales were never sent (F123 note; Part D § 5 lists a full restore by an administrator as not defended). The pass signal is kept as the target; the spike is expected to **fail (b)** unless a mitigation is added (OQ27). Candidates to test, none sourced yet: a monotonic high-water mark outside the database (TPM NV counter where a TPM exists; a DPAPI-protected value an administrator could still restore); the highest local sequence reported in every lease renewal and heartbeat (helps only if the device was online after the sales); reuse of printed receipt numbers; cash and stock reconciliation at day close | Linux phase 3: (a), (b). Windows phase 4: (c). **Dev runs (2026-10-18/19, tier E, spike README):** (a) PASS under the OQ7 policy (SC-13 + TS-01a: a reused number quarantines the identity, nothing lost). (b) **expected blind spot confirmed**: a restore after offline sales that never reached the server leaves no trace on the server. Measured mitigations in the TS-01b leg: TS-10 drawer count (catches it only while the cash stays in the drawer), the OQ27 offline-duration alert, the hourly USB copy (bounds a lost PC to the unsynced sales after the last copy) | sync |
| TS-02 | **Outbox deletion by an insider with the database key.** **(a)** Delete one queued command before sync. **(b)** Delete one command and renumber the later commands and the local counter, leaving no gap. | The server detects the sequence gap and alerts the owner. | (a): the gap is refused by the compare-and-set cursor (B2-A, F124, F126). The device then cannot advance past it, so the server raises a "missing sequence N" alert to the owner once the gap outlives a configured time; nothing after the gap is applied twice or silently. **(b): same blind spot as TS-01(b)** — no gap is visible to the server; the target pass signal is kept and the expected result is fail (OQ27) | Linux phase 3: (a), (b). **Dev run PASS (2026-10-18, tier E, spike README):** (a1) a deleted queued command whose bill is retained is rebuilt from the bill after a resend request: no loss, no alert (finding P3-F13). (a2) founder decision 2026-10-18: commands after the gap are stored and held, never refused, as in the OQ7 quarantine. The dashboard shows "held: N sales, X EGP" with the alert "missing sequence N, possibly deleted on the device" once the gap outlives a threshold; the owner accepts the gap, the lost number is recorded permanently and the held commands apply once. (b1) caught at start by the commit marker (tamper `store_older_than_marker`). (b2) renumbering with the marker forged by the key holder: **expected blind spot confirmed** (OQ27). | sync |
| TS-03 | **Edited encrypted file.** Flip bytes in database pages, the WAL, the rollback journal (if used) and the file header, with the app closed and while it runs. | The app detects it, refuses to use the corrupted data (never writes over it), recovers from the server and records the event; unsynced commands lost in the corrupted region surface as a reported sequence gap, never silently. | SQLCipher per-page HMAC and `cipher_integrity_check` (desktop-note F101, F104); SQLite3MC protection depends on the cipher chosen (desktop-note F110); WAL-frame coverage by either engine is **NOT SOURCEABLE — test**. Recovery uses bootstrap E (C2) and the gap report of TS-06. The damaged file is kept aside as evidence | Linux phase 1: (a) detect and refuse. Linux phase 3: (b) recover from the server and report the gap. Windows phase 4: (c) repeat through the chosen runtime binding (desktop S10) | sync. **Linux phase 3 (b) dev run PASS (2026-10-19, tier E, spike README S206 leg):** the damaged store is kept aside unchanged, the device re-bootstraps, and the unsynced commands in it are reported as a lost range with an owner alert |
| TS-04 | **Copied to another PC or Windows user.** Copy the database (with its wrapped key blob) to another PC, and open it as another Windows user on the same PC; scan the database, `-wal`, `-shm`, journal, temp directory and crash dumps for plaintext. | It cannot be opened; no plaintext appears in the database, WAL, journal or temp files. | (a) Plaintext: whole-file encryption plus `temp_store=MEMORY`, because SQLite temp files are not covered by SQLCipher encryption (desktop-note F101); the scan uses canary strings planted in test sales. (b) Key binding: DPAPI user scope cannot be unwrapped on another PC or by another user (desktop-note F112). **DPAPI machine scope (K2) can be unwrapped by any user on the same PC (desktop-note F112), so the "another Windows user" leg passes only with K1 user scope or a TPM-sealed key (K3)** — this feeds the key decision in desktop-note S11 | Linux phase 1: (a). Windows phase 4: (b), with desktop-note S11 | sync |
| TS-05 | **Wrong key or brute force.** Open with a wrong raw key, a truncated key, an empty key and repeated attempts. | Fails safely; no partial plaintext. Also: the app never treats a failed open as a new install, never recreates or overwrites the file, and records the event. | SQLCipher/SQLite3MC refuse to read with a wrong key. SQLite3MC's `PRAGMA key` reports success even with a wrong key, so the app must verify with a read (desktop-note F110). A raw 256-bit key makes offline brute force impractical; a PIN-derived key (K5) does not, so K5 must never be the only factor (desktop-note § 4.3.2 4b) | Linux phase 1: (a). Windows phase 4: (b) through the chosen runtime binding | sync |
| TS-06 | **Database file deleted**, with the app closed and while it runs. | The app re-bootstraps from the server. The server's sequence check detects and reports any unsynced data loss — never silent. | Bootstrap E (C2). The server compares the last highest local sequence the device reported (`sync_cursor_lag`, B § 8; lease renewals, D2-B) with its cursor and reports the commands lost. If the device never reported after those sales, the server cannot count them, so every re-bootstrap with possible unsynced data raises an owner alert carrying the last-contact time. The device identity must survive the deletion (kept outside the database) or the device must be re-enrolled with owner approval. Deleting an open file behaves differently on Linux (unlink succeeds) and Windows (sharing rules; **NOT SOURCEABLE — test**) | Linux phase 3: (a) closed, (b) unlink while running. Windows phase 4: (c) delete while running | sync. **Linux phase 3 (a), (b) dev run PASS (2026-10-19, tier E, spike README S206 leg):** (a) deleted while closed: the unsynced commands are reported as a lost range; (b) deleted while running: SQLite refuses the next write (finding P3-F14), the device re-bootstraps in-process, nothing accepted and nothing lost |
| TS-07 | **Clock change during an open session** (forward and back, while the app runs). | Session time is billed from the trusted time source, not the edited wall clock; the edit is recorded. | D1-B: elapsed time from the monotonic reading, which ignores clock edits (desktop-note F117); D1-I integer billing (F325); the wall-versus-monotonic divergence flag and both times on every event (D3-B); security event 4616 where the app can read it (F306, S304) | Linux phase 3: (a) injected clock on the session and billing logic (with SC-11). **(a) dev run PASS (2026-10-18, tier E, spike README):** the bill follows the monotonic counter whatever the wall clock does; each move above 5 min is recorded once with its size (owner event); an app closure is measured on the monotonic counter. Finding P3-F12: measured on the wall clock, a closure with the clock set back raised no notice. Windows phase 4: (b) real clock edits with S304 and S306 | N5/time |

**Expected outcome to plan for:** TS-01(b) and TS-02(b) are not covered by the current design. An insider with file access (TS-01b) or the database key (TS-02b) can erase offline sales that never reached the server. Until OQ27 is decided in the sync threat model, a failure on those two variants is the honest result.

**Candidate mitigations for OQ27** (founder, 2026-10-16 — recorded for the sync `/threat-model`, **not decided**). Whatever risk remains after M1–M3 is a residual risk the founder accepts by name in that threat model.

| # | Candidate | What it changes | Limits (inference unless cited) | Spike check |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| M1 | **File access control (primary).** The database, its WAL, journal, `-shm` and wrapped key blob live in a directory ACL'd to a separate Windows service account; the cashier's Windows user cannot read, copy or replace them. The install checklist keeps cashier accounts non-admin. | TS-01(b) and TS-02(b) then require administrator rights on the venue PC. | **Architecture consequence:** if only the service account can read the files, the cashier app cannot open the database in its own process. Storage and sync must run in a separate process under that account (desktop option E2 helper or E3 service), and the UI must talk to it over local IPC. This conflicts with the desktop note's E1 lean (in-process). E3 behaviour, session-0 isolation and DPAPI under a service account are NOT SOURCEABLE so far (desktop-note F119; S12). An administrator still has full access. | **TS-08** (Windows, phase 4; extends desktop-note S11 and TS-01c) |
| M2 | **Offline-period alerts.** The server records every gap in device contact during the venue's configured business hours; the owner sees "counter offline from X to Y". | Deliberate disconnection is a precondition of TS-01(b), so the attack window becomes visible to the owner. | The alert shows *when* the counter was offline, not *what* was sold. Honest outages trigger it too, which is visibility, not an accusation. Precision is bounded by the contact interval (push J polling, C2). | **TS-09** (Linux, phases 2–3) |
| M3 | **Shift cash reconciliation** (compensating control): drawer count vs recorded cash sales at shift close (idea-pool W1). | Erased cash sales whose cash is still in the drawer show up as a surplus. | **Does not catch the usual TS-01(b) theft:** a cashier who erases sales and also removes their cash leaves a drawer that matches the restored records. Café stock counts (idea-pool I1) may still show the missing items; erased station time leaves no physical trace. | **TS-10** (Linux, phase 3) |
| M4 | **Anti-rollback marker outside the database**: TPM monotonic counter where a TPM exists; device reporting its highest local sequence on every contact. | Could detect a restore even when the erased sales never reached the server. | **Research candidate, not assumed:** TPM counter behaviour, availability (some venue PCs have no TPM, desktop OQ3) and admin resistance are unresearched. A highest-sequence report helps only if the device made contact after the sales. | none until a `/research` run supports it; TS-01(c) then tests it |

**Spike checks added for M1–M3:**

| ID | Scenario | Pass signal | Legs | Feeds |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| TS-08 | **Non-admin cashier versus the database files.** As a standard (non-admin) cashier user, try to read, copy, open, rename, delete, replace and restore the database, WAL, journal, `-shm`, wrapped key blob and their directory, including through Explorer, the command line, Previous Versions and backup tools. Repeat as an administrator to mark the boundary. | Every attempt by the cashier user is denied, and the counter keeps working normally for that user (principle 2). The administrator's success is recorded as the residual boundary, not as a pass. | Windows phase 4, with desktop-note S11 and TS-01c; needs the storage process running under the service account (M1) | sync |
| TS-09 | **Offline-period alert.** The harness cuts the device's network for planned intervals inside and outside the configured business hours, including one interval during which it sells and then restores the database (TS-01b). | Every planned interval inside business hours appears once in the server record and in an owner alert, as "counter offline from X to Y" with X and Y within one contact interval of the true times. No interval is missing. Intervals outside business hours are recorded without an alert. | Linux phase 2 (alert mechanics), phase 3 (combined with TS-01b) | sync |
| TS-10 | **Shift reconciliation after an erased sale.** Run TS-01(b), then close the shift with a counted drawer: (a) the cash of the erased sales left in the drawer; (b) that cash removed. | (a) The shift close flags a surplus equal to the erased cash sales, recorded as an owner-visible event. (b) Expected **not** detected by M3; the run records this as M3's limit, and the case falls to M1, M2 and the residual risk. | Linux phase 3 | sync. **Dev run (2026-10-19, tier E, spike README):** (a) the shift close flags a surplus equal to the erased cash as one owner event ("drawer surplus of X EGP at shift close"). (b) not detected, as expected: M3's limit |

#### 9.1.2 Added cases (founder, 2026-10-16)

Added after phase 2 passed, before phase 3 starts. They extend Part E's scenario list (SC-01–SC-18, 5.1.5) and are judged by the same invariants I1–I10. Mechanisms are design proposals (inference) unless an F-number is cited. "Phase" follows the table in 9.1. **SC-19 is the highest-risk gap and runs first in phase 3.**

**Device retention rule under test (founder, 2026-10-16; provisional values in OQ29: 60 days on the device, prune only below the verified-restore watermark and older than 60 days; server backup window 30 days, set by the founder 2026-10-18, ADR-0005 Decision 6).** An outbox row is deleted once a fully parsed ack for it arrives (spike finding P2-F1: never on a status code). The business records behind it stay on the device: bill, payment and session minutes, with their command id, sequence and payload hash. They stay for a bounded window longer than both ETA's 30-day late-submission route (ETA note F317) and the server's backup/PITR window, and are pruned only after the server confirms that its archive holds them. The spike runs this rule with placeholder values, so SC-19 can show whether it is enough.

| ID | Scenario | Pass signal | Mechanism under test | Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SC-19 | **Server restored from backup or PITR after devices hold acknowledged commands.** The device syncs, gets acks and deletes those outbox rows. The server database is then restored to a point before some of those acks (full backup and PITR, with restore points before, inside and after a batch), and the device reconnects. Variants: (a) the lost range is inside the device's retention window; (b) part of it is older than the window; (c) the device sells offline during the restore; (d) the restore also rewinds the server-to-device feed and config versions. | Every action acknowledged before the restore is on the server **exactly once** after reconnect (I1, I2). The server **detects** the gap itself on first contact, without relying on the device's goodwill. It records a "server behind device" event with the lost sequence range and alerts the owner. Commands rebuilt from retained records carry the **same command id, sequence and payload hash** as the originals. (b) The part beyond the window is reported as an exact, unrecoverable sequence range, never silently. (d) The device does not apply a feed or config version that went backwards as if it were new, and does not lose its own newer facts. | The device reports its highest acknowledged sequence H on every contact (proposal). The server compares H with its restored cursor N; if N < H it answers "resend from N+1". The device rebuilds commands N+1..H from retained business records, and the server applies them as new because its inbox lost them too. A restore point cannot split a batch, because the batch is one server transaction. Side effects the server sent after the restore point (ETA submissions, owner alerts) must be re-sent under the same idempotency keys. How ETA treats a resubmitted receipt UUID is open (ETA note F310, F325). Retention rule above; OQ29 | 3 (first) |
| SC-20 | **Disk full (ENOSPC)** (a) during the "close and pay" transaction (journal creation, page writes, COMMIT); (b) during the commit-marker write (temp file, fsync, rename). Also the ack-mark write (folds in SC-17). | (a) The sale fails **visibly** (the cashier is told it was not saved) and leaves no partial rows: never a bill without its outbox row, or the reverse (I1). The app issues an explicit `ROLLBACK` (F443), `integrity_check` is ok, and selling resumes once space is freed. (b) The committed sale stays acknowledged and is synced. The store is ahead of the marker, which is the safe direction, so the next start does not falsely refuse it. The app records a "marker behind" event and retries until the marker catches up once space is freed. No sale lost or duplicated in either case. | `SQLITE_FULL` covers the main file and temp files (F444); partial-statement rollback semantics (F443); OQ28 A + B marker rules (README phase 1). Injection: an apsw VFS shim returning `SQLITE_FULL` or `SQLITE_IOERR` at the n-th write (deterministic), plus one real-filesystem leg on a size-capped filesystem (for example a Docker `--tmpfs` with a size limit) | 3 |
| SC-21 | **App update with an unsynced outbox and a local schema migration.** (a) Commands are pending when the new version migrates the local schema; a kill hits at every migration step (before, mid-migration, after, before the marker). (b) Windows leg: the installer replaces the binaries and runs the migration under the real updater. | After any kill the store is either fully old or fully new schema, and `integrity_check` is ok. Every pending command is sent **byte-identical** (same command id, sequence and payload hash), so server dedupe still works across versions (I2). The server accepts the older command-envelope version. The commit marker stays valid. A copy of the pre-migration store is kept until the migration is verified (as in P1-F3). A downgrade below the minimum version is refused without touching the store (OQ21). | Extends SC-18. Stored payload bytes are sent verbatim, never re-serialised by the new code (proposal; OQ14 serialisation). A versioned command envelope. Migrations reversible in development, forward-only in production (coding standards) | 3: (a). 4: (b). **Windows migration half of (b) (desktop spike round 4 G5.1, commit `4dfc169`, `prototypes/desktop-client-spike/evidence/g5/run-log-g5-20261019.txt`):** a power cut at each of the 6 migration steps, 24 of 24 cuts PASS in both runtimes; the schema never mixed, the queued rows' stored bytes unchanged, the pre-migration copy removed only after the verify, and the old app refused the newer store without touching it (G5-F1). Migration time on the throttled disk: runtime A 0.68–4.05 s (control 2.08 s), runtime B 0.24–0.87 s (control 0.24 s); only A can pass the 3 s ceiling (G5-F2). **Founder (2026-10-19, G5 verdict):** no physical 4 GB PC exists, so the VM's slow-disk profile is the proxy and A's 4.05 s the worst case known; the risk closes on the first pilot café's own PC before a release reaches it. **New local-store requirement:** a migration that can exceed 3 s shows an "updating" screen with progress, never a frozen window. Still for phase 4: the byte-identical send and server dedupe across versions after the real update |
| SC-22 | **Two app instances started at once on the same store**: simultaneous start, N times; both selling; both syncing; one killed mid-transaction. | Only one instance holds the store: the second refuses to start, or waits and says so. If both ever get in, no sequence number is used twice, no sale is lost, and the commit marker never moves backwards. No `SQLITE_BUSY` reaches the cashier as a lost sale. | A single-instance lock outside SQLite, held for the process lifetime (proposal). `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` serialises writers (5.1.2). The marker write must only ever advance (compare-and-advance under the lock): two processes each doing temp-file-plus-rename could leave the lower value last. The server side of concurrent sends already passed (phase 2, SC-05) | 3 |
| SC-23 | **Owner changes a price or config on the server while the device is offline with a session running.** Changes: the station's hourly price per tier and a café item price. The device closes the session and sells café items offline, then syncs. | **Product rules (founder decisions, 2026-10-16), which replace the per-sale review flag first planned here:**<br>**(1)** A running session keeps the prices frozen at its start (G12). A price-tier change during the session (idea-pool N4) uses the price list the device has at that moment. A session with several segments is rounded **once on its total**, never per segment, to the owner's rounding step (OQ8, founder 2026-10-17). The spike takes the step from the list held at close; the billing spec confirms which list applies.<br>**(2)** A sale made at the last price the device knew is a valid fact. The server accepts it with **no** per-sale "needs review" flag. **Widened (founder, 2026-10-17):** the owner sees **one summary per period in which the device lacked the current price list**, whether it was offline or simply had not pulled the new list yet. A sale is judged only by its `cfg_version` against the list in force on the server, never by device time. The period runs from the server's publication of the next version to the device's first pull of a newer one, both in server time. Offline periods inside it are context for the message: "N sales at the previous price while the counter lacked the current list from X to Y (offline from … to …)".<br>**(3)** A new price takes effect on the device when the device receives it, never retroactively. The server never re-prices a recorded sale.<br>**(4) (founder, 2026-10-17)** A device that has never received a price list, or whose list lacks the price needed, refuses to start a session or sell, with a clear error. It never bills at zero.<br>**Pass signal:**<br>- Every bill equals a model computed independently from the price list the device held at each moment: session-start list for frozen segments, current list at a tier change, current list for café items.<br>- Every fact carries the config version it used.<br>- Server amounts equal device amounts, with no re-pricing.<br>- Exactly one summary per lacking period, with the right count and bounds, offline or online, and no per-sale flags.<br>- With no price list, or a price missing: refused with `no_price_list` / `price_missing` and nothing billed.<br>- A store damaged while running is still refused when a session operation is the first to read the damaged page.<br>- Device and server totals agree at day close (brief F3).<br>**Consequence of judging by version only (spike finding P3-F6):** a sale made while its version was still current, but uploaded after the next version was published, also counts as "at the previous price". The server cannot tell the two apart without trusting device time. **Decision (founder, 2026-10-17):** keep judging by `cfg_version` only. The owner summary may show the device's sale time as context, labelled **unverified**, and never uses it to decide. Message wording: "N sales billed at a price list that was no longer current when they reached the server (device times, unverified: …)".<br>**(5) For the billing and config spec (founder, 2026-10-17):** the dashboard refuses to publish a price list that lacks a required setting (a tier price, the rounding step, the minimum minutes) and names what is missing. The device's `no_price_list` / `price_missing` refusal stays as the last defence, not the normal path.<br>**Not in SC-23 (founder, 2026-10-16):** permission revocation while offline (abuse case 3) is a separate later cell. Its rules come from the staff auth and roles research, which has not run yet. | Part C option C-1: immutable versioned config, the server is the only writer, facts carry the version, open sessions keep the price frozen at start. S1 session events. Offline periods come from the TS-09 contact-gap record (M2). Billing rules (rounding, increments) are placeholders until OQ8 | 3 |
| SC-24 | **Start-up integrity check time** on a store sized for the device retention window (OQ29, OQ16), on the HDD-throttled Windows VM, cold cache. | Measured: the time of `integrity_check`, `quick_check` and a full page-authentication read at three store sizes, up to the OQ16 bound over OQ29's 60 days (about 300,000 money commands; derived from capacity, not measured). Each result is reported with size, hardware and cold/warm state, and is indicative only. Pass: within the start-up budget in `docs/architecture/perf-budgets.md` (founder, 2026-10-19): ready to sell within 10 s of app start, of which the integrity check takes at most 5 s. | Options to compare if it is too slow; each re-runs TS-03a, because each weakens "detect before the first write": (1) `quick_check` at start, O(N) instead of O(N log N), skipping UNIQUE and index-content checks (F445); (2) a partial check of the hot tables (outbox, recent bills) at start with `integrity_check(TABLENAME)` (F445), and the full check in the background or at day close; (3) the full check only after an unclean shutdown (hot journal present, or store and marker disagree) and on a schedule, relying on the cipher's per-page authentication on every read (desktop-note F101, F110); (4) a smaller live store, with records past the window archived out (OQ29) | 4 |
| SC-25 | **(a) Days-long offline backlog:** the device sells offline for 7 simulated days at the OQ16 bound (about 35,000 money commands; derived from capacity, not measured). **Added (founder, 2026-10-17):** the backlog drains over several short online windows, each cut mid-drain, as at shift changes. **(b) N5 grace expires mid-shift** with sessions open and sales unsynced. | (a) The backlog drains in sequence order in bounded batches, exactly one effect each (I1, I2), across every window cut, including a cut inside a batch and after the server committed a batch whose reply was lost. Each window makes progress, so no window restarts from the beginning. Nothing is applied twice and nothing is skipped. A batch too large for the HTTP timeout shrinks instead of forming a retry loop. The counter keeps selling during the drain (principle 2; latency is measured in SC-26). The drain time is measured. Receipts past ETA's 24-hour deadline go to the late-submission route and are flagged (ETA note F317). (b) Whatever expiry blocks (decided in OQ3), open sessions can still be closed and billed. No sale or session minute is lost or hidden, the expiry is recorded, and everything syncs once the device reconnects. | Ordered batches with reject-record-advance (§ 5). D2 lease and grace (Part D; OQ3, OQ4). An injected clock for the expiry | 3: (a), and (b) mechanics with an injected clock. 5: (b) with the real N5 rules and a tampered clock |
| SC-26 | **Counter read latency while sync writes**, under rollback-journal mode (OQ28 A). The counter reads open sessions, bills and the catalogue while sync marks acks, a backlog drains and the marker is fsynced. | Measured: p95 and p99 of the counter's reads with and without sync writing, on the HDD-throttled VM. No `SQLITE_BUSY` reaches the cashier. Pass: within the counter budget (principle 2). Budget set in `docs/architecture/perf-budgets.md` (founder, 2026-10-19): p95 ≤ 100 ms, p99 ≤ 300 ms, max ≤ 3 s, and the cashier never sees `SQLITE_BUSY`. | In rollback-journal mode a commit needs an EXCLUSIVE lock and waits for current readers, and new readers wait behind its PENDING lock (F446). WAL avoided this; OQ28 gave WAL up. If it fails: shorter write transactions (smaller ack batches), a single writer process that owns the store (fits M1 and desktop E2/E3), or an in-memory read model for the counter | 4 |
| SC-27 | **Antivirus (Windows Defender real-time scanning) holding a lock** on the database, rollback journal, marker or the marker's temp file during commit, journal deletion and marker rename. Plus a full scan running during a sales burst. | Over N commits: 0 lost or duplicated sales and 0 false refusals. A failed journal delete or marker rename is retried or fails visibly, never silently. The latency impact is measured. Any Defender exclusion the installer would need is listed with its security cost, for the sync threat model. | How Windows file sharing behaves under scanning: NOT SOURCEABLE — not yet searched (Microsoft Defender docs, SQLite's Windows VFS retry behaviour). Candidate settings to compare: `journal_mode=TRUNCATE` or `PERSIST` (no journal delete per commit) against `DELETE`. Either change must re-run phase 1's tamper and kill cells | 4 |
| SC-28 | **Dashboard edit of an open bill or session (OQ9, founder 2026-10-17).** The owner edits an open bill from the dashboard: (a) with the device connected; (b) with the device offline; (c) racing the cashier closing that bill; (d) with the connection cut after the edit is sent and before the device confirms. | (a) The edit reaches the device as a command; the device applies it once and confirms; the dashboard shows "pending" until then, then "applied". (b) The dashboard refuses the edit; nothing is queued. (c) If the bill closed first, the edit is rejected visibly and the closed bill is unchanged. Any change after that is a correction command (void or adjustment) that keeps the original. (d) A resent edit is applied at most once (idempotent command id), and "pending" ends in "applied" or "rejected", never stuck. | A server-to-device command channel with idempotent command ids and a confirmation back. The server is the writer of the edit request, the device of the bill. Corrections are new records (OQ6). Once an e-receipt exists, the correction is an ETA Return Receipt (not modelled in the spike) | 3 |
| SC-29 | **Stock policy per item (OQ9, founder 2026-10-17).** Items set to "allow negative stock and alert" and items set to "refuse the sale". The last unit is sold offline (and twice across a restore), and the owner changes the policy while the device is offline. | "Allow and alert": the sale goes through, the stock goes negative, and the owner is alerted once per item and offline period. "Refuse": the sale is refused at the counter with a clear message and nothing is billed. A new item defaults to "allow and alert". A policy change applies when the device receives it (as SC-23 rule 3). | Stock counts and the per-item policy on the config feed; refusal decided on the device from the policy it holds | 3 |
| SC-30 | **Device off while a session runs (OQ8 rule 3, founder 2026-10-17).** The cashier PC is powered off (or the app killed) with sessions open, for minutes and for hours, then restarted. | Session time keeps counting across the outage, so the bill includes it. At restart the owner sees "device was off from X to Y" once. No session minute is lost or counted twice. | Elapsed time from the session start anchor (D1-B); the outage bounds come from the device's last-alive checkpoint and the restart, so they rest on device time. Their trust is the clock question of Part D | 3: mechanics with an injected clock; 5: with tampered clocks |
| SC-31 | **End Day (OQ8 rule 4, founder 2026-10-17).** The cashier presses End Day (a) with nothing open; (b) with an open bill; (c) with a running session; (d) with an open shift; (e) offline, then syncs; (f) while a kill hits the End Day write. | (a) The business day closes, and the next opens on the next action. (b)–(d) End Day is refused with a clear message naming what is open; nothing closes. Every bill's money and every session's minutes belong to the business day in which the bill was closed and paid, and a session and its bill always share one business day. (e) The offline End Day reaches the server once, in order; day-close totals agree (brief F3). (f) After restart the day is either closed or still open, never half. | End Day as an ordinary money command in the device sequence (same outbox and dedupe); the business day is a device counter, not a calendar date | 3 |

##### [A] Desk check before S2–S4 (2026-10-19, founder request; nothing downloaded)

The founder asked for a desk check, with nothing downloaded, before any PowerSync download, because ADR-0001 (Accepted) forbids any `BYPASSRLS` role, runtime or not. Sources: PowerSync docs (fetched 2026-10-19) and the service source at tag `v1.27.0` (commit `45955b534245c4bf1527229cb3b1113a505e2f64`), read through the GitHub API.

**(1) Is `BYPASSRLS` mandatory for the replication role?**
- **PowerSync's code does not enforce it.** At start the service checks each published table. If RLS is on and the role is neither superuser nor `BYPASSRLS`, it logs warning `PSYNC_S1145` and carries on (`modules/module-postgres/src/replication/WalStream.ts` lines 285–290: "We log the message, then continue anyway"). The error catalogue recommends `ALTER ROLE powersync_role BYPASSRLS`, with "explicit policies for the replication role" as the alternative (`packages/service-errors/src/codes.ts`). The setup docs create the role `WITH REPLICATION BYPASSRLS` ([source DB setup](https://docs.powersync.com/configuration/source-db/setup.md)).
- **How it reads:**
  - The initial snapshot is a plain `SELECT * FROM <table>` through a cursor (`SnapshotQuery.ts` lines 26–44), so RLS applies to it.
  - Live changes come from a logical slot (`CREATE_REPLICATION_SLOT … LOGICAL pgoutput`, `WalStream.ts` line 569) on the one publication, which must be named `powersync` ([source DB setup](https://docs.powersync.com/configuration/source-db/setup.md)).
  - PostgreSQL 18: a replication connection needs the `REPLICATION` attribute, "a very highly privileged role" ([CREATE ROLE](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/sql-createrole.html)). Publisher row security policies "can execute" when the role lacks `SUPERUSER` and `BYPASSRLS` ([logical replication security](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/logical-replication-security.html)). Whether RLS filters the decoded change stream is not stated: **NOT SOURCEABLE**, the pages read do not say.
  - A publication row filter is fixed per table, takes only simple immutable expressions on columns, and is the same for every subscriber ([row filters](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/18/logical-replication-row-filter.html)). It cannot carry a per-tenant session value.
- **Under ADR-0001 it is mandatory in effect.**
  - ADR-0001 Decision 1 puts a RESTRICTIVE `tenant_isolation` policy with no `TO` clause on every tenant table. It binds every role, and permissive policies are ORed but restrictive ones ANDed, so no "explicit policy for the replication role" can reach past one tenant.
  - The predicate reads `app.tenant_id`. One PowerSync connection serves every tenant and sets no tenant, so its snapshot returns **no rows** (inference from ADR-0001's predicate and the plain `SELECT`).
  - The only ways for its snapshot to see every tenant are `BYPASSRLS` or superuser (forbidden by ADR-0001 Decision 1b and its "Must never"), or a policy exemption for its role (forbidden by check C2: the exact predicate on every role).
  - One PowerSync instance per tenant, each fixing `app.tenant_id` in its connection options, would need one replication slot and one service per tenant. Its change stream is not shown to be filtered by RLS. Rejected as unworkable on one VPS (inference).
  - Separately, a logical `REPLICATION` login role is not in ADR-0001's closed role inventory. Only `backup_rt` has `REPLICATION`, for physical backup.
- **So:** `BYPASSRLS` is a recommendation in PowerSync, not a code requirement. With Kodeebo's schema under ADR-0001, though, PowerSync can sync nothing unless its role bypasses RLS or the restrictive tenant policy exempts it. Either one breaks ADR-0001.

**(2) What the advisories leaked and how they were fixed.** All three are in `@powersync/service-core`; 1.27.0 has all three fixes.
- **GHSA-q6wc-xx4m-92fj** (CVE-2026-30870, moderate, CVSS 6.5, CWE-285; affected `=1.20.0`, fixed in 1.20.1 / sync-rules 0.33.0): on sync streams with `config.edition: 3`, a subquery that only decides **whether a table syncs at all** was not applied. Example: `SELECT * FROM sensitive_table WHERE auth.user_id() IN (SELECT user_id FROM admins)` synced the table to every authenticated user. Row-partitioning subqueries, edition 2 and `bucket_definitions` were not affected. Fix: PR #547 "Sync Streams: Fix bugs around expanding request filters" (merged 2026-03-05). Over-synced data is removed from devices at their next connection.
- **GHSA-hfrf-pvrg-5c3h** (moderate, CVSS 4.3, CWE-285; affected `>=1.20.0 <1.26.0`, fixed in 1.26.0): on edition-3 streams, a JOIN matching **two or more non-unique fields** could sync row combinations the filters should have excluded, for example `a.x = b.x AND a.y = b.y AND b.k = c.k AND c.user_id = auth.user_id()`. Fix: PR #780 "Fix provenance for lookups with multiple inputs" (merged 2026-09-01). Whether another user's or tenant's rows could be exposed is not stated (confidentiality rated Low).
- **GHSA-6wfp-847m-28x5** (high, CVSS 7.5, CWE-248; affected `<1.24.0`): crafted messages on an unauthenticated WebSocket crash the process, and repeats sustain the denial of service. WebSockets are on in every instance. The code change is not described.
- **Supporting evidence for the ADR-0001 point:** two of the three are authorization bugs in the sync-rules layer within six months. With PowerSync, that layer would be the only tenant boundary for synced data, since its role bypasses RLS.

**(3) Extra containers, memory, licence** ([deployment architecture](https://docs.powersync.com/maintenance-ops/self-hosting/deployment-architecture), [self-hosted config](https://docs.powersync.com/configuration/powersync-service/self-hosted-instances)).
- **Processes:** a replication process (`start -r sync`), API processes (`start -r api`), a daily `compact` job (up to 1 GB), migrations; plus **bucket storage**, either MongoDB (a replica set, even with one node) or PostgreSQL (≥ 14 may share the source server; storage version 2, no version 4 or object storage).
- **Smallest documented setup:** one container for API and replication with "512MB memory, 1 vCPU", plus one MongoDB replica-set node with "2GB memory, 1 vCPU", about **2.5 GB more** on the 8 GB VPS.
- **Documented production setup:** a 1 GB replication container, 2+ API containers at 1 GB each, and a 3-node MongoDB replica set at 2+ GB each: **about 9 GB, more than the whole VPS**.
- **Connection limit:** each API container is "limited to 200 concurrent connections", with about 100 recommended ("1x PowerSync API container per 100 connections").
- **Licences:** the service is **FSL-1.1-ALv2** (LICENSE at `v1.27.0`; a "Competing Use" bar, each version turning Apache-2.0 after two years, F10). The Kotlin SDK `com.powersync:common-jvm` 1.15.2 declares MIT in its Maven POM; its GitHub repo has no LICENSE file. `powersync-sqlite-core` is Apache-2.0.

**Founder decision (2026-10-19): PowerSync is rejected by ADR-0001. `BYPASSRLS` is treated as mandatory in effect: not required by PowerSync's code, but needed for it to sync anything under ADR-0001.** The advisories in (2) are supporting evidence. S2–S4 are not run, and S1 and S5 lapse with them, so the PowerSync track is closed. OQ1 (adopt vs build) is answered "build" for PowerSync. The other engines (OQ24) were not assessed against ADR-0001. **Reopen only if** the founder reopens ADR-0001. Nothing was downloaded.

| S201 | Cursor probe under load: many concurrent writers, one long transaction; measure feed delay with scheme B and horizon-age alerting | No row skipped; delay bounded by the longest transaction | Cursor choice |
| S202 | Push through the real proxy and a real venue line: SSE and polling, heartbeat interval, silent connection death, router reboot, wake from sleep; measure time to detect a dead stream | Client recovers by cursor pull with no lost change in every case | Push transport |
| S203 | Two simulated offline devices on one branch against a test server: same station, last stock item, duplicate numbers, day close with one device behind | Every conflict flagged, no fact lost or counted twice | C3 table |
| S204 | Scheme C (tenant counter row lock): confirm sequence order equals commit order, measure contention and gaps after rollback | Order equals commit order; contention acceptable | Cursor fallback |
| S205 | Fault injection on batch apply: cut the network mid-batch, resend, duplicate replays, crash after commit before ack | Zero lost or duplicated facts over N randomized runs (brief risk 1) | Dedup design (with parts B and E) |
| S206 | Re-bootstrap: device with local data loss and unsynced outbox; device offline past the tombstone window | No resurrected deletes; unsynced facts never silently discarded. **Dev run PASS (2026-10-19, tier E, spike README):** a lost or damaged store is moved aside and re-bootstrapped; the server records what it never received as a permanent lost range (exact from the commit marker; without it, an alert that later sales cannot be counted) with one owner alert. The config feed is applied as a snapshot (finding P3-F15: upsert kept deleted items sellable). **Founder decision (2026-10-19): a device that lost its store while offline stays refused until it reaches the server, as today; it never continues offline at the marker's sequence.** Reasons: (1) the staff PIN verifiers lived only in that store (ADR-0007), so nobody can sign in, and re-enrolment needs the server anyway; (2) continuing at the marker's sequence would mask the OQ27 erase case, while refusing makes the server compare the marker's sequence with the last one it received and report the gap at once; (3) a phone hotspot is enough to reach the server, and until then the café writes paper bills, which the End Day count reconciles | Bootstrap and tombstones |
| S207 | Old client against a newer server: unsupported protocol and schema versions | Client keeps selling and queuing; applies no feed it cannot read. **Dev run PASS (2026-10-18, tier E, spike README):** below the server's minimum protocol nothing is applied and the queue stays on the device, with one owner alert ("this device needs an app update before it can sync; its sales are kept on the device"); a feed with a newer schema is not applied and the held prices stay in force; after the update the backlog applies once and the feed is read | Versioning |

### 9.5 Queries and evidence hygiene

All five parts report that their searches and fetches named only public products, standards, papers and vendors — no secrets, customer data or internal hostnames. Evidence scripts start throwaway local containers with a throwaway password and bind to localhost.
